yields the following character sum identity

(6)

pH_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\},\{1,1,\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}\};1\right)=\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)a_{p}(f_{6.4.a.a})+\left(\frac{-2}{p}\right)p.

More can be said when the triangle group
\Gamma

is a matrix group
not containing
-I_{2}
, such as
\Gamma=(3,\infty,\infty)\cong\Gamma_{1}(3)
and
\Gamma=(\infty,\infty,\infty)\cong\Gamma_{1}(4)
, see §
3
for more details. In these cases we obtain explicit Hecke trace formulas for almost all
p
and all
k\geq 2
. To describe our result,
recall the identity
(see for example
[
25
]
)

(7)

\sum_{i=0}^{k}u^{i}v^{k-i}=\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}(-1)^{j}\binom{k-j}{j}(uv)^{j}\cdot(u+v)^{k-2j}.

Theorem 2
.

For
\Gamma=~(e_{\infty},\infty,\infty)
with
e_{\infty}\in\{3,\infty\}
, let
HD(3,\infty,\infty)=\{\{\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}\},\{1,1\}\}
and
HD(\infty,\infty,\infty)=\{\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\},\{1,1\}\}
. Denote by
N_{\Gamma}
the level of
\Gamma
as a subgroup of
SL_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
, which is
3
(resp.
4
) for
e_{\infty}=3
(resp.
\infty
).
Choose the generator
\lambda=\lambda(\Gamma)
of
\mathbf{Q}(X_{\Gamma})
so that
\lambda
takes values
\infty,1,0
at the vertices of
X_{\Gamma}
of order
e_{\infty}
,
\infty
,
\infty
, respectively. Then for all integers
k\geq 1
, the contributions of the right hand side of (
1
) at
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}\setminus\{0,1\}
are given by

(8)

\sum_{j=0}^{k/2}(-1)^{j}\binom{k-j}{j}p^{j}\cdot H_{p}\left(HD(\Gamma);1/\lambda\right)^{k-2j},

The contributions at
\lambda=0,1,\infty
are distinguished in two cases:

(1)

For
\Gamma_{1}(4)
, they are
1,\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)^{k},\frac{1+(-1)^{k}}{2}
, respectively.

(2)

For
\Gamma_{1}(3)
, those from
\lambda=0
and
1
are
1
and
\left(\frac{-3}{p}\right)^{k}
, and from
\lambda=\infty
it is

\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }p\equiv-1\mod 3,\quad k\equiv 1\mod 2;\\
(-p)^{k/2}&\text{ if }p\equiv-1\mod 3,\quad k\equiv 0\mod 2;\\
(-1)^{k}\displaystyle\sum_{\overset{0\leq i\leq k}{k\equiv 2i\mod 3}}p^{i}\cdot J_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3}\right)^{k-2i}&\text{ if }p\equiv 1\mod 3,\end{cases}

where
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}}=\langle\omega\rangle
and
J_{\omega}(a,b)=\sum_{x\in\mathbf{F}_{p}}\omega^{(p-1)a}(x)\omega^{(p-1)b}(1-x)
.

In particular, when
p\equiv-1\mod N_{\Gamma}
and
k
is odd,
\mathrm{Tr}(T_{p}\mid S_{k+2}(\Gamma))=0
.

Explicit Hecke trace formulae have many applications. For instance, knowing Hecke trace can lead to special values of hypergeometric functions. As an example, it follows from the reasoning in Yang
[
72
]
that the Hecke trace
a_{7}(h_{4}^{2})
above yields the identity

\,{}_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{4}&\frac{3}{4}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\frac{5}{6}&\frac{7}{6}\end{matrix}\;;\;\frac{2^{10}\cdot 3^{3}\cdot 5^{6}\cdot 7}{11^{4}\cdot 23^{4}}\right]=\frac{11\cdot 23}{140\sqrt{3}}\frac{2^{1/3}(4+2\sqrt{2})}{7^{7/6}}\frac{\Gamma(7/6)\Gamma(13/24)\Gamma(19/24)}{\Gamma(5/6)\Gamma(17/24)\Gamma(23/24)}.

The recent paper
[
4
]
provides an explicit hypergeometric-modularity method to express certain types of hypergeometric character sums in terms of Hecke eigenvalues of modular forms based on the ideas in this paper. Its sequel
[
5
]
explores some applications of the method. The trace formulae in this paper are used by Grove in
[
32
]
to obtain the vertical Sato-Tate distribution, as
p\to\infty
, of the normalized
H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);\lambda),\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}
, for
\Gamma
in Theorem
2
. The distribution of that for
\Gamma
in Theorem
1
can be obtained by a similar idea. The paper
[
33
]
studies the distribution of certain normalized hypergeometric character sums attached to data of length 3 and 4.

We end this section with an outline of the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
, and the roles of the remaining sections. The trace formulae, and automorphic sheaves
V^{k}(\Gamma)
over both the complex and
\ell
-adic fields are discussed in §
2
. In particular, Proposition
3
and Remark
2.2.3
reduce the computation at generic points to
k=2
for Theorem
1
and
k=1
for Theorem
2
, respectively.
For certain arithmetic triangle groups
\Gamma
, it follows from the Rigidity Theorem
5
and the Comparison Theorem
8
in §
3
that the automorphic sheaves
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
or
V^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
, up to twists by rank-1 sheaves,
could be replaced by hypergeometric sheaves for which the Galois action on a stalk has Frobenius traces explicitly expressed by hypergeometric character sums. In §
3.3

we identify such
\Gamma
whose associated hypergeometric data are defined over
\mathbb{Q}
; they are the groups in Theorems
1
and
2
.
We recall in §
4
the hypergeometric sheaves and derive their basic properties, following the works of Katz
[
40
]
and Beukers-Cohen-Mellit
[
16
]
. Using the information obtained, we determine, for each
\Gamma
, the required twist of the corresponding
\ell
-adic hypergeometic sheaf so that the resulting sheaf is isomorphic to the automorphic sheaf
V^{i}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
for
i=2
or 1, as appropriate. This gives the contributions from generic points. The contributions from singular points are the theme of §
5
.
All elliptic points are CM points. We first determine the CM structure at each elliptic point, with results summarized in Table
8
in §
5.1
. Then in §
5.2
we compute the contribution at
p
from each elliptic point, which mainly depends on the order of the point and the behavior of
p
in the CM field. The contribution from each cusp is computed in §
5.3
. For
k
even it is 1, as done in Scholl
[
60
]
, while for
k
odd, it is
\pm 1
or 0, depending on the type of reduction at
p
of the degenerate elliptic curve at the singular point. This completes the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
. In §
6
we provide an independent verification of the Hecke trace formula for
S_{k+2}(\Gamma)
with smallest
k\geq 1
by proving the hypergeometric character sum identities directly. The two novelties mentioned in §
1.1
are addressed in §
7
. The Appendix at the end clarifies the
G
-Sheaves and the definition of the pushforward sheaves appearing in this paper.

2.
Automorphic sheaves

2.1.
Complex automorphic sheaves

2.1.1.

For a Fuchsian subgroup
\Gamma
of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
of first kind,
let
X_{\Gamma}:=X_{\Gamma}^{an}=\Gamma\backslash\mathfrak{H}^{*}
,
where
\mathfrak{H}^{*}=\mathfrak{H}
or the union of
\mathfrak{H}
and the cusps of
\Gamma
according to whether
\Gamma
is cocompact or not.
Let
p:\mathfrak{H}^{*}\to X_{\Gamma}
be the projection.
Note that
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
carries a canonical topology, and
p
is a continuous map.
In this paper, two kinds of
\Gamma
are considered:

1.

\Gamma
is commensurable with
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
. Then the cusps of
\Gamma
is the set
of rational numbers and the point
\infty
.

2.

\Gamma
is commensurable to the set
O_{B}^{1}
of norm 1 elements in a maximal order
O_{B}
of an indefinite quaternion algebra
B

over
\mathbf{Q}
. That is, we choose once and for all an embedding
B\subset M_{2}(\mathbf{R})
, which induces an embedding

\theta:O_{B}^{1}\subset\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
. In this case, the set of cusps is empty, so
\mathfrak{H}^{*}=\mathfrak{H}
.

We also have the set of elliptic points of
\Gamma
. We have a finite set

S=S_{c}\cup S_{e}\subset X_{\Gamma}
of points which are cusps or elliptic points,
that is, their preimages in
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
are cusps or elliptic points of
\Gamma
, respectively. Let

X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}=X_{\Gamma}-(S_{c}\cup S_{e}),\quad\quad Y_{\Gamma}=X_{\Gamma}-S_{c}=\Gamma\backslash\mathfrak{H},

the complement of the set of cusps and elliptic points, and the complement of the
set of cusps, respectively. Note that

p:\mathfrak{H}-\{\text{elliptic points of \ }\Gamma\}\to X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}

is a covering space in the sense of topology. In particular, if
\Gamma
has no elliptic points,
then
p:\mathfrak{H}\to Y_{\Gamma}
is the universal covering of
Y_{\Gamma}
.
The action of
\Gamma
on
\mathfrak{H}^{*}
is via the quotient

\bar{\Gamma}
in
\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
. For the groups in this paper,

\bar{\Gamma}=\Gamma/\Gamma\cap\{\pm I\}
. Therefore, if

\Gamma
is torsion-free, then

p:\mathfrak{H}\to Y_{\Gamma}
is the universal covering of
Y_{\Gamma}
, and
the fundamental group at any base-point
x

\pi_{1}(Y_{\Gamma},x)\cong\Gamma,

an isomorphism unique up to inner automorphism. In the quaternion cases
this is
\pi_{1}(X_{\Gamma},x)\cong\Gamma
for
\Gamma
without torsion.
In general, there is an epimorphism

\pi_{1}(X^{\circ}_{\Gamma},x)\to\bar{\Gamma}\cong\mathrm{Aut}((\mathfrak{H}-\{\text{elliptic points of \ }\Gamma\})/X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}).

We consider local systems of
\mathbf{C}
-vector spaces of finite dimension on
X^{\circ}_{\Gamma}
and their extensions to constructible
sheaves on
X_{\Gamma}
. By the usual dictionary, a local system on
X^{\circ}_{\Gamma}
is equivalent to a finite-dimensional representation
of
\pi_{1}(X^{\circ}_{\Gamma},x)
. In this paper, all the local systems will be defined by representations of
\bar{\Gamma}
.

2.1.2.

There are constructible sheaves of
\mathbf{C}
-vector spaces
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}

on
X_{\Gamma}
for integers
k\geq 1
with the following properties:

1.

Over the open subset
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}

these automorphic sheaves are local systems of rank

k+1
. They are thus solution sheaves to a system of regular singular differential equations
on
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
.
For
\Gamma
considered in this paper, these will be hypergeometric local systems when
k=1
or 2.

2.

The sheaves
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
on
X_{\Gamma}
are extensions of the sheaves on
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
defined above:

V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}=\iota_{*}(V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}\mid X_{\Gamma}^{\circ})\quad\text{for~the~inclusion~map}~\iota:X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}\to X_{\Gamma}.

3.

Fundamental theorems of Eichler, Shimura and Kuga give:

H^{1}_{par}(\Gamma,\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2}))\cong H^{1}(X_{\Gamma},V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}})\cong S_{k+2}(\Gamma)\oplus\overline{S_{k+2}(\Gamma)},

which is a Hodge decomposition of type
(k+1,0),(0,k+1)
.
The left-hand side is parabolic cohomology.
Moreover

H^{1}(X_{\Gamma},V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}})\cong\mathrm{Im}(H^{1}_{c}(X_{\Gamma}^{\circ},V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}})\to H^{1}(X_{\Gamma}^{\circ},V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}))

the image of the compactly supported cohomology.
The Hecke operators act on the spaces as geometric correspondences and these
isomorphisms are equivariant for the Hecke actions.

When
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
is an arbitrary Fuchsian subgroup of the first kind, these sheaves
are discussed in detail in
[
13
]
by Bayer and Neukirch. The group
\Gamma
acts on

\mathfrak{H}^{*}\times\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2})
, on the first factor by fractional linear transformations,
on the second factor via
\varrho_{k}:=\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\varrho_{1})
,
where
\varrho_{1}

arises from the inclusion
\Gamma\subset\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
and the canonical action of

\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
on
\mathbf{C}^{2}
. Then

(9)

V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}:=p_{*}^{\Gamma}(\underline{\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2})})=\Gamma\backslash\mathfrak{H}^{*}\times\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2}),

where the middle expression uses the
\Gamma
-invariant push-forward functor. See the Appendix on
G
-sheaves for the theory of the functor
p_{\ast}^{G}
. Note that
if
-I\in\Gamma
these sheaves are identically zero if
k
is odd. When
k
is even, the
action factors through the projective group
\bar{\Gamma}=\Gamma/\{\pm I\}
, and this action is discontinuous.
When
\Gamma
is torsion-free, Bayer and Neukirch establish the Eichler-Shimura isomorphisms
with cusp forms for all
k
, even or odd. When
\Gamma
has torsion and
k
is even, they show that by going to a torsion-free
normal subgroup
\Gamma^{\prime}
of finite index
and using the projection
q:X_{\Gamma^{\prime}}\to X_{\Gamma}
,

V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}=q_{*}^{\Gamma/\Gamma^{\prime}}\left(V^{k}(\Gamma^{\prime})_{\mathbf{C}}\right)

gives the Eichler-Shimura isomorphism. When
x\in X_{\Gamma}
is not a cusp or elliptic point,
the stalk
(V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}})_{x}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2})
. For
x
a cusp
or elliptic point,

(V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}})_{x}=\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\mathbf{C}^{2})\right)^{\Gamma_{\tau}}

where
\tau\in\mathfrak{H}^{*}
is any point with
p(\tau)=x
and
\Gamma_{\tau}
is the stabilizer of
\tau
in
\Gamma
.
The same holds for
k
odd and
\Gamma
not containing
-I
.

2.2.
\ell
-adic automorphic sheaves

2.2.1.

For
\Gamma
as before, in this section we view the quotient
X_{\Gamma}
as the collection of complex points of a curve over a number field.
Recall that
Y_{\Gamma}=\Gamma\backslash\mathfrak{H}
, which is
X_{\Gamma}
for
\Gamma
cocompact, and
X_{\Gamma}
with cusps removed for
\Gamma
non-cocompact.
Let
G
be the algebraic group
\textsf{GL}_{2}
or
\textsf{B}^{\times}
according as
\Gamma
non-cocompact or cocompact arising from an indefinite quaternion algebra
B
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.
In both cases,
G(\mathbf{R})=
GL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
which has two connected components
G(\mathbf{R})_{\pm}
consisting of elements
with positive and negative determinants, respectively. Moreover,
G(\mathbf{R})_{+}
acts transitively on the upper half plane
\mathfrak{H}
via fractional linear transformations so that we may identify
\mathfrak{H}
with
G(\mathbf{R})_{+}/\rm{SO}_{2}Z(\mathbf{R})
, where
Z
denotes the center of
G
. Thus
G(\mathbf{R})/\rm{SO}_{2}Z(\mathbf{R})
may be identified with
\mathfrak{H}^{\pm}
, the disjoint union of upper and lower half plane. Let
G(\mathbf{Q})_{+}=G(\mathbf{Q})\cap G(\mathbf{R})_{+}
. Write
\mathbb{A}
for the ring of adeles over
\mathbf{Q}
, and
\mathbb{A}_{f}
the subring of finite adeles. The group
G(\mathbf{Q})
is diagonally embedded in
G(\mathbb{A})
. Denote by
G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}
the diagonal imbedding of
G(\mathbf{Q})_{+}
in
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

Let
U
be a compact-open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.
Shimura’s theory of canonical models gives a curve
Y_{U}
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
whose complex points can be expressed as

\displaystyle Y_{U}:=G(\mathbf{Q})\backslash\mathfrak{H}^{\pm}\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U=G(\mathbf{Q})_{+}\backslash\mathfrak{H}\times G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U.

It has finitely many connected components, parametrized by the double cosets

G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/U=\coprod_{i=1}^{h}G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}\,x_{i}U

with
h=|\widehat{\mathbf{Z}}^{\times}/\det U|
. Note that an element
\gamma\in G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}
satisfies
\gamma x_{i}U=x_{i}U
if and only if
\gamma
lies in
\Gamma_{i,U}:=G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}\cap x_{i}Ux_{i}^{-1}
, a congruence subgroup of SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
. Thus we get

(10)

\displaystyle Y_{U}=\coprod_{i=1}^{h}\Gamma_{i,U}\backslash\mathfrak{H}=\coprod_{i=1}^{h}Y_{\Gamma_{i,U}},

showing that each connected component of
Y_{U}
is the curve of a congruence subgroup of SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
. Conversely, all congruence subgroups
\Gamma
of SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
are known to arise as
\Gamma=G(\mathbf{Q})_{+,f}\cap U
for some compact open subgroup
U
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
. We call
U
the
(adelic) group corresponding to
\Gamma
.

Definition 1
.

A compact open subgroup
U
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
is called neat if all
\Gamma_{i,U}
defined above are torsion-free.

Note that, when
h>1
, the curve
Y_{U}
while defined over
\mathbf{Q}
is not absolutely irreducible over
\mathbf{Q}
.
Its connected components

Y_{\Gamma_{i,U}}
are absolutely irreducible over an abelian extension of
\mathbf{Q}
given by
Shimura’s theory of canonical models.

Adelically, we may regard

Y_{U}=G(\mathbf{Q})\backslash G(\mathbf{R})G(\mathbb{A}_{f})/{\rm{SO_{2}Z}}(\mathbf{R})U=G(\mathbf{Q})\backslash G(\mathbb{A})/{\rm{SO_{2}Z}}(\mathbf{R})U.

Denote by
S_{k}(U)
the space of automorphic forms on
G(\mathbb{A})
, cuspidal if
G=\text{GL}_{2}
, right invariant under
U{\rm Z}(\mathbf{R})
, whose components at the place
\infty
lie in the space of discrete series representation of
G(\mathbf{R})
of weight
k
.
Note that it decomposes into the direct sum
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{h}S_{k}(\Gamma_{i,U})
.

In the elliptic modular cases there are canonical compactifications
X_{U}
of
Y_{U}
by adding cusps of
\Gamma_{i,U}
. For the
quaternion cases, we set
X_{U}=Y_{U}
.

Finally, there are constructible
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
sheaves
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
to be elaborated in the next subsection.
For both cases Eichler-Shimura theory takes the shape:

Theorem 3

(Deligne
[
21
]
, Ohta
[
54
]
)
.

For integers
k\geq 0
, the action of the Galois group
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
of
\mathbf{Q}
on
H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U)_{\ell})
is unramified at the primes
p\neq\ell
where
X_{U}
has good reduction. Moreover, we have

\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p}\mid H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U)_{\ell}))=\mathrm{Tr}(T_{p}\mid S_{k+2}(U)).

Combined with the Grothendieck-Lefschetz trace formula, we can express the trace of
T_{p}
on
S_{k+2}(U)
at primes
p
where
X_{U}
has good reduction as a sum over the
\mathbf{F}_{p}
-points on the reduced curve:

(11)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{Tr}(T_{p}\mid S_{k+2}(U))=\sum_{\lambda\in X_{U}(\mathbf{F}_{p})}\mathrm{Tr}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\lambda}\mid(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}).

Write
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
for the restriction of the sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{\Gamma}
. In case that
X_{\Gamma}
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
, we have
X_{U}=X_{\Gamma}
and
S_{k+2}(U)=S_{k+2}(\Gamma)
. Then the above theorem gives a geometric description of the trace of the Hecke operator
T_{p}
on
S_{k+2}(\Gamma)
.

Now we explain the notation
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}
on the right side of (
11
). Let
X_{U}^{\circ}
be the complement in
X_{U}
of the cusps and elliptic points. Then our
sheaves
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
are lisse on the open set
X_{U}^{\circ}
, equivalent therefore
to a representation of
\pi_{1}(X_{U}^{\circ},\bar{\eta})
where
\bar{\eta}
is
the generic geometric point

\bar{\eta}=\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}(X_{U})})
of
X_{U}^{\circ}
.
Thus there is a canonical epimorphism

G_{\eta}:=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}(X_{U})}/\mathbf{Q}(X_{U}))\to\pi_{1}(X_{U}^{\circ},\bar{\eta})

so that we may regard all lisse sheaves on
X_{U}^{\circ}

as modules for the Galois group
G_{\eta}
.
Every algebraic point
t\in X_{U}
gives a discrete valuation of the function field
\mathbf{Q}(X_{U})
with residue field
\kappa(t)
, so we may
speak of the decomposition
D_{t}
and inertia
I_{t}
subgroups (well-defined up to conjugation) and their
images in
\pi_{1}(X_{U}^{\circ},\bar{\eta})
.
As
t:\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa(t))\to X_{U}
gives a morphism of étale sites, we get

t^{*}V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
, a sheaf for the étale site on
\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa(t))
.
This is equivalent to a module over
G_{\kappa(t)}:=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\kappa(t)}/\kappa(t))\cong D_{t}/I_{t}
for an algebraic closure
\overline{\kappa(t)}
of
\kappa(t)
. In other words, the stalk
(
V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{t}}
is the
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-vector space
\bar{t}^{*}V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
with
G_{\kappa(t)}
action. Here
\bar{t}:\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{\kappa(t)})\to\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa(t))\to X_{U}
.

In particular, if
t
is in the open set
X_{U}^{\circ}
, then the inertia
I_{t}
acts trivially. Also,
by choosing an embedding
\kappa(\bar{t})\subset\kappa({\bar{\eta}})
, we get a homomorphism

G_{\kappa(t)}=\pi_{1}(\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa(t)),\bar{t})\to\pi_{1}(X_{U}^{\circ},\bar{\eta})

and any representation of the right-hand side gives a representation of the left hand side. This
describes
the stalk
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{t}}
for

t\in X_{U}^{\circ}
.

Moreover
if
\iota:X_{U}^{\circ}\hookrightarrow X_{U}
is the inclusion, then our sheaves also have the property that

V^{k}(U)_{\ell}=\iota_{*}(V^{k}(U)_{\ell}\mid X_{U}^{\circ}).

This implies that for every

t\in X_{U}
, the stalk
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{t}}
is the
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-space
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta}}^{I_{t}}
with
G_{\kappa(t)}
action.

The structure of these stalks will be examined in more detail in §
2.2.3
.

The curves
X_{U}
are defined over
\mathbf{Q}
. In fact, they have canonical
reductions modulo all but finitely many primes
p
. This follows because of the interpretation of these curves
as moduli spaces. That is, we may regard
f:X_{U}\to S
where
S=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{Z}[1/N])
for an integer
N\geq 1
,
where
f
is smooth and projective. Moreover, the sheaves
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
extend to these integral models, and
for each
i\geq 0
, the sheaves
R^{i}f_{*}\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
are lisse on
S
and compatible with base-change. This implies
that for each prime
p\nmid N
we have an isomorphism

H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{F}}_{p},V^{k}(U)_{\ell}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{F}}_{p})\cong H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U)_{\ell}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{Q}}),

respecting Galois actions. Namely, a Frobenius
\mathrm{Frob}_{p}\in G_{\mathbf{Q}}
acts on the left-hand side as the
(inverse of the)
p
th power endomorphism.
The Grothendieck-Lefschetz
trace formula expresses the Frobenius trace as a sum of local traces in the fibers of the points

X_{U}(\mathbf{F}_{p})
.

For
\lambda\in X_{U}(\mathbf{F}_{p})
in (10), the stalk
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}
refers to the stalk of
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
at any algebraic point
t
of
X_{U}
which reduces to
\lambda
modulo
p
.

2.2.2.

As in the previous subsection, let
G
be
GL
2
or
\textsf{B}^{\times}

for an indefinite quaternion algebra
B
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
. Let
U
be an open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.
In this subsection, we discuss the
\ell
-adic sheaves
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
, following the construction by Ohta in
[
54
]
. Since it is crucial to this paper, we sketch the key idea below.

Denote by
\varrho_{k}:\textsf{GL}_{2}\to\textsf{ GL}_{k+1}
the morphism of algebraic groups over
\mathbf{Z}

which is
k
th symmetric power of
\varrho_{1}:\textsf{ GL}_{2}\to\textsf{ GL}_{2}
,
the identity map. So for any commutative ring
R
,
\varrho_{k}(R):\rm{GL}_{2}(R)\to\rm{GL}_{k+1}(R)

sends the matrix
A\in\rm{GL}_{2}(R)
to the
R
-linear transformation

R^{k+1}\to R^{k+1}
given by the matrix which is
the
k
th symmetric power of
A
.

When
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
, choose a real quadratic field
F
which splits
B
, namely
B\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}F=\rm{M}_{2}(F)
, and fix an embedding
\theta:B\to{\rm{M}}_{2}(F)
. Since
B
is an inner form of
{{\rm{M}}}_{2}(F)
, there is an element
a\in\rm{GL}_{2}(F)
so that the embedded image can be described as

\theta(B)=\{x\in{\rm{M}}_{2}(F):a\,x\,a^{-1}=x^{\tau}\},

where
\tau
is the nontrivial automorphism of
F
over
\mathbf{Q}
.
Hence every
x\in\theta(B^{\times})
satisfies

{\varrho_{k}}(a){\varrho_{k}}(x){\varrho_{k}}(a)^{-1}={\varrho_{k}}(x)^{\tau},\quad{\rm for~all}~k\geq 1.

Let

Y_{k}=\{x\in{\rm{M}}_{k+1}(F):{\varrho_{k}}(a)\,x\,{\varrho_{k}}(a)^{-1}=x^{\tau}\}\subset{\rm{M}}_{k+1}(F).

It is a central simple algebra over
\mathbf{Q}
with an involution. By construction

Y_{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}F\cong{\rm{M}}_{k+1}(F).

The invertible elements of
Y_{k}
define
an algebraic group over
\mathbf{Q}
, denoted
\textsf{Y}_{k}^{\times}
. Note that

\textsf{Y}_{k}^{\times}(\mathbf{Q})=Y_{k}^{\times}
and
\textsf{Y}_{k}^{\times}(F)=(Y_{k}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}F)^{\times}\cong{\rm GL}_{k+1}(F)
.
Let
j:Y_{k}^{\times}\hookrightarrow{\rm{M}}_{k+1}(F)^{\times}=\mathrm{GL}_{k+1}(F)
be the natural inclusion.
There is a morphism of algebraic groups

\varrho_{k}^{\prime}:\textsf{B}^{\times}\to\textsf{Y}_{k}^{\times}
with the property that

j\circ\varrho_{k}^{\prime}(\mathbf{Q}):\textsf{B}^{\times}(\mathbf{Q})=B^{\times}\to\textsf{Y}_{k}^{\times}(\mathbf{Q})=Y_{k}^{\times}\hookrightarrow{\rm GL}_{k+1}(F)

is
\theta
restricted to
B^{\times}
followed by
\varrho_{k}(F)
, and
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}(F)
is
\varrho_{k}(F)
. Thus if we identify
B^{\times}
with
\theta(B^{\times})\subset{\rm GL}_{2}(F)
, then we may regard
\varrho^{\prime}_{k}(\mathbf{Q})
as the restriction of
\varrho^{\prime}_{k}(F)
to
B^{\times}
.

As such, there are two possibilities for
Y_{k}
:

(I)
Y_{k}={\rm{M}}_{k+1}(\mathbf{Q})
,

(II)
Y_{k}={{\rm{M}}}_{(k+1)/2}(D)
for a (division) quaternion algebra
D
over
\mathbf{Q}
.

For
k\geq 2
even,
Y_{k}
falls in case (I) and
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
is a
(k+1)
-dimensional representation of
the algebraic group
\textsf{B}^{\times}
.

In the second case, we can use the regular representation
r:D^{\times}\to\mathrm{GL}_{4}
and
define a representation
\textsf{B}^{\times}\to\textsf{GL}_{2(k+1)}
by composing

\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
with the embedding

{{\rm{M}}}_{(k+1)/2}(D)\to{\rm{M}}_{(k+1)/2}({\rm{M}}_{4}(\mathbf{Q}))={\rm{M}}_{2(k+1)}(\mathbf{Q}).

Note that
Y_{1}=\theta(B)=D
falls in case (II). So
\varrho_{1}^{\prime}
is 4-dimensional.

Fix a positive integer
k
. Let
g
be an element in
G(\mathbf{Q})
such that
U\subset g\prod_{p}G(\mathbf{Z}_{p})g^{-1}
. Then
\Gamma=G(\mathbf{Q})_{+}\cap U
is contained in
gG(\mathbf{Z})g^{-1}
. In the representation space of
\varrho_{k}
(resp.
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
) if
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
(resp.
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
), choose a lattice
L_{k}
stable under
\varrho_{k}(gRg^{-1}\cap G(\mathbf{Q}))
(resp.
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}(gRg^{-1}\cap G(\mathbf{Q}))
), where
R={\rm{M}}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
for
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
and
R
is a maximal order
O_{B}
for
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
.

Fix a prime
\ell
.
Define a tower of curves

...\rightarrow Y_{U_{3}}\rightarrow Y_{U_{2}}\rightarrow Y_{U_{1}}\rightarrow Y_{U_{0}}=Y_{U}

where

U_{n}=U\cap g(\{x\in G(\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}):x\equiv id\mod\ell^{n}\}\prod_{p\neq\ell}G(\mathbf{Z}_{p}))g^{-1}.

Then each
Y_{U_{n}}
is a finite Galois covering of
Y_{U}
with Galois group

\mathrm{Aut}(Y_{U_{n}}/Y_{U})=U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U_{n}.

As a compact subgroup of the discrete group
{\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})
,
{\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U
is contained in
\{\pm\mathrm{id}\}
for
U
in general, and is trivial for
U
neat. It follows from our choice of
g
that the finite group
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U_{n}
can be represented by elements in
gRg^{-1}\cap G(\mathbf{Q})
.
The constant group-scheme
Y_{U_{n}}\times L_{k}/\ell^{n}L_{k}
has an action
of
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U_{n}
. On the first factor this is the Galois action resulting from the theory of canonical models; the action on the second factor is from
\varrho_{k}
or
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
as appropriate.

Assume
U
is neat as in Definition
1
. The quotient

V_{n}(U,L_{k}):=(U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U_{n})\backslash(Y_{U_{n}}\times L_{k}/\ell^{n}L_{k})

is
a finite étale group-scheme over
Y_{U}
.
These form a projective system with
n
. Taking projective limit yields a lisse
\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}
-sheaf
V(U,L_{k})
on
Y_{U}
. Tensoring this
with
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
, we obtain a lisse
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-sheaf on
Y_{U}
independent of the choice of
L_{k}
. The sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
is the pushforward of this sheaf on
Y_{U}
via the inclusion map
\iota:Y_{U}\hookrightarrow X_{U}
. Recall that
\iota
is surjective for
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
, and for
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
, the complement
X_{U}\setminus Y_{U}
is the set of cusps. Restricting
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
to
X_{\Gamma}
gives the sheaf
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
.

When the group
U
is neat, the curve
Y_{U}
admits a moduli interpretation, namely there is a family of abelian varieties
f:A_{U}\to Y_{U}
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
such that each fiber is an elliptic curve for
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
, and a polarized abelian surface for
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
.
The sheaves defined above can be expressed geometrically as well. As shown in
[
55
, Theorem 3.3.3]
,

(\varprojlim_{n}V_{n}(U,L_{1}))\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}\simeq R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}

and hence we can make the identification

V^{1}(U)_{\ell}=\iota_{*}R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell},\quad\iota:Y_{U}\hookrightarrow X_{U}.

Therefore
V^{1}(U)_{\ell}
is a rank-2 or 4 sheaf according as
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
or
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
. In the elliptic modular case, since
\varrho_{k}=
Sym
{}^{k}(\varrho_{1})
, we have

V^{k}(U)_{\ell}=\iota_{*}\mathrm{Sym}^{k}R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell},

as given by Deligne
[
21
]
. Thus
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
has rank
k+1
. For the quaternion case, as discussed before,
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
has rank
k+1
for
k
even, but
the relation between
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
and
R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
is less clear. Proposition (4.3.1) of
[
55
]
shows that
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
occurs as a component of the sheaf
R^{w}f_{*}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
for suitable choices of
w
and
n
, whose Galois module structure
can be expressed as

R^{w}f_{*}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\bigwedge^{w}R^{1}f_{*}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}=\bigwedge^{w}(R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})^{n},

in which the first equality follows from the structure of the cohomology of abelian varieties and the second from the Künneth formula.
From this one sees that all the sheaves
R^{w}f_{*}^{n}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
are calculated from tensor operations from the
fundamental
V^{1}(U)_{\ell}
. We will see an expression for
V^{2k}(U)_{\ell}
similar to the elliptic modular case in Proposition
3
.

The sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
defined above is for
U
neat. For a general
U
, there is a neat compact open normal subgroup
U^{\prime}
of
U
(such as
U_{n}
above for
n
large) for which
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
is defined. It follows from the definition of
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
that its stalk at any algebraic point of
Y_{U^{\prime}}
is
(\varprojlim_{n}L_{k}/\ell^{n}L_{k})\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
, on which
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
acts since by construction the lattice
L_{k}
is invariant under the action of
\varrho_{k}
or
\varrho_{k}^{\prime}
on
gRg^{-1}\cap G(\mathbf{Q})
and the quotient
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
can be represented by elements in
gRg^{-1}\cap G(\mathbf{Q})
. We say that
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
admits
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
-structure. This allows us to define
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
by pushforward from
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
as described below. Here
k
is even if
{\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U=\{\pm{\rm id}\}
so that
\varrho_{k}(-{\rm id})=(-{\rm id})^{k}={\rm id}
.

As before, let
X_{U}^{\circ}
be the open subset of
X_{U}
with cusps and elliptic points removed. Denote by
T_{U^{\prime}}
the preimage of
X_{U}^{\circ}
under the covering map
p:X_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U}
. Note that
T_{U^{\prime}}\subset X_{U^{\prime}}^{\circ}
. Let
q:T_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U}^{\circ}
be the restriction of
p
to
T_{U^{\prime}}
. Then
q
is finite étale. When
U
is neat, we have
X_{U}^{\circ}=Y_{U}
and
T_{U^{\prime}}=Y_{U^{\prime}}=X_{U^{\prime}}^{\circ}
. The sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
restricted to
Y_{U}
defined before is the pushforward of
V^{k}(U^{\prime})
restricted to
Y_{U^{\prime}}
via the covering map
q:Y_{U^{\prime}}\to Y_{U}
. Hence, as a generalization from neat to general
U
, we pushforward the restriction of
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
to
T_{U^{\prime}}
via
q
to get a lisse sheaf
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
on
X_{U}^{\circ}
, and define
the sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
as the pushforward of
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
via the inclusion
\iota:X_{U}^{\circ}\to X_{U}
. In other words,
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}=\iota_{*}q_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}(V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}\mid T_{U^{\prime}})
. Note that the sheaf
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
on
X_{U}^{\circ}
is independent of the choice of
U^{\prime}
, hence so is
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
.

Lemma 1
.

The sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
defined above agrees with the pushforward
p_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
via the covering map
p:X_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U}
. Here
k
is even if
{\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U=\{\pm{\rm id}\}
.

Proof.

By construction, this is true at all points of
X_{U}^{\circ}
. Let
t
be a point
of
X_{U}\setminus X_{U}^{\circ}
and
t^{\prime}
a point of
X_{U^{\prime}}
lying over it.
Because
U^{\prime}
is a normal subgroup of
U
with finite index,
p
is a Galois cover with Galois group

U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
, which acts transitively on each fiber of
p
.
Denote by
\bar{\eta}
and
\bar{\eta^{\prime}}
the generic geometric points of
X_{U}
and
X_{U^{\prime}}
respectively.
Note that

U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}\cong\mathrm{Aut}(X_{U^{\prime}}/X_{U}).

Also, because we defined
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
via
\iota
,
and
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
is lisse on
X^{\circ}_{U}
,
we have

(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{t}}=(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta}}^{I_{t}}.

Similarly, we also have

(V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})_{\bar{t}^{\prime}}=(V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta}^{\prime}}^{I_{t^{\prime}}}.

Here
I_{t}
and
I_{t^{\prime}}
are the inertia subgroups of the decomposition groups
D_{t}
and
D_{t^{\prime}}
at
t
and
t^{\prime}
, respectively. The stabilizer of
t^{\prime}
in
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
is
D_{t}/D_{t^{\prime}}
with the inertia subgroup
I(t^{\prime}/t)=I_{t}/I_{t^{\prime}}
. Since the stalk at
t
of
p_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
is
((V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})_{\bar{t^{\prime}}})^{I(t^{\prime}/t)}
the result now follows from

((V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta^{\prime}}}^{I_{t^{\prime}}})^{I_{t}/I_{t^{\prime}}}=((V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta^{\prime}}}^{I_{t}}=(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\eta}}^{I_{t}}.

∎

Observe that the sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
defined above is compatible with taking the first cohomology.

Proposition 1
.

With the above notation and assumption on the parity of
k
we have

H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}=H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},p_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})=H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U)_{\ell}).

Proof.

Because
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
is flat over
\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}
it follows from the definition of
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
that

H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}=H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V(U^{\prime},L_{k}))^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Z}_{\ell}}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}.

An elementary argument shows that

\displaystyle H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V(U^{\prime},L_{k}))^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=(\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V_{n}(U^{\prime},L_{k})))^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V_{n}(U^{\prime},L_{k}))^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}},

so the problem is reduced to prove that

H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V_{n}(U^{\prime},L_{k}))^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}\cong H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},p_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}V_{n}(U^{\prime},L_{k})).

For
\ell
prime to the order of
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
, the higher
direct images
R^{i}p_{*}^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}(\mathcal{F})=0
for
i>0
and any sheaf
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathbf{Z}/\ell^{n}
-modules. A spectral
sequence argument then gives the result (in the case of ordinary sheaf cohomology,
see
[
31
, ch. V, cor. of prop. 5.2.3, and th. 5.3.1]
).
∎

In
[
54
]
Ohta proved Theorem
3
for
U
neat using the sheaf
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
. For a general
U
, he chose a neat compact open normal subgroup
U^{\prime}

of
U
for which Theorem
3
holds, then took the
U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}
invariant part of both sides to show that the trace of the Hecke operator
T_{p}
on
S_{k+2}(U)
is equal to the trace of Frob
p
on
H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}
. Proposition
1
above implies that

H^{1}(X_{U^{\prime}}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell})^{U/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U)U^{\prime}}
indeed is equal to
H^{1}(X_{U}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\bar{\mathbf{Q}},V^{k}(U)_{\ell}).

Remark 1.
When the group
\Gamma
is a finite index subgroup of
O_{B}^{1}
for a split or nonsplit indefinite quaternion algebra
B
over
\mathbf{Q}
, the group of Atkin-Lehner involutions is defined as the quotient
\Gamma^{*}/(\{\pm id\}\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma^{*}
is the normalizer of
\Gamma
in the group of elements in
O_{B}
with positive reduced norm. It is a finite elementary 2-group. A split
B
corresponds to
G={\textsf{GL}}_{2}
. When
\Gamma
is
\Gamma_{0}(N)
or
\Gamma_{1}(N)
, these are the familiar Atkin-Lehner operators
W_{Q}
in the literature (cf.
[
7
,
8
]
), where
Q
is the highest power of a prime
p
dividing
N
. A nonsplit
B
corresponds to
G={\textsf{B}}^{\times}
. When
\Gamma=O_{B}^{1}
, the group
\Gamma^{*}/(\{\pm id\}\Gamma)
is generated by
r
elements, which can be chosen to be any element in
O_{B}
with reduced norm
p
at the
r
places
p
where
B
ramifies (see §28.9 of
[
69
]
). More discussions are given in §5.1. We shall regard the Atkin-Lehner involutions as elements in
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
.

Let
U
be the open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
corresponding to
\Gamma
and let
U^{\prime\prime}
be the group generated by
U
and some Atkin-Lehner operators of
\Gamma
. Let
U^{\prime}
be a neat normal open compact subgroup of
U
. Then the same argument as before shows that the sheaf
V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
admits
U^{\prime\prime}/({\rm Z}(\mathbf{Q})\cap U^{\prime\prime})U^{\prime}
-structure so that the sheaf
V^{k}(U^{\prime\prime})_{\ell}
on
X_{U^{\prime\prime}}
can be defined as the pushforward
q_{*}^{U^{\prime\prime}/({\rm Z(\mathbf{Q})}\cap U^{\prime\prime})U^{\prime}}V^{k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
via the projection
q:X_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U^{\prime\prime}}
.

For
U^{\prime\prime}
defined above, when the curve
X_{U^{\prime\prime}}
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
, Theorem
3
also holds for
U^{\prime\prime}
.

2.2.3.

The goal of this subsection is to investigate the Galois structure of the stalks of
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
. We begin by proving a general statement for the quaternion case inspired by Theorem 3.1.2 of
[
9
]
.

Proposition 2
.

Let
B
be a quaternion algebra over
\mathbf{Q}
and
K
an extension of
\mathbf{Q}
such that
B_{K}=B\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}K\cong{\rm M}_{2}(K)
. Let
V_{K}
be a 4-dimensional
K
-vector space which is a principal
B_{K}
-module. Suppose a group
H
acts on
V_{K}
via the representation
\rho_{K}
which commutes with the
B_{K}
-action.
Then, with the standard action of
{{\rm{M}}}_{2}(K)
on the
2
-dimensional
K
-vector space
K^{2}
, we have

(1)

W_{K}:=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{M}_{2}(K)}(K^{2},V_{K})=\mathrm{Hom}_{B_{K}}(K^{2},V_{K})

is a 2-dimensional
K
-vector space endowed with the
H
-action via the
representation
\sigma_{K}
induced from the
H
-action on
V_{K}
;

(2)
\rho_{K}\cong\sigma_{K}\oplus\sigma_{K}
as
H
-modules, and
\rho_{K}\cong\sigma_{K}\otimes\varrho_{1}(K)
as
H\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}(K)
-modules.

Proof.

The induced
H
-action on
W_{K}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{M}_{2}(K)}(K^{2},V_{K})
is given by the rule

(\sigma_{K}(h)f)(z)=\rho_{K}(h)(f(z))\quad{\rm for}~h\in H,f\in W_{K},~{\rm and}~z\in K^{2}.

We verify that
\sigma_{K}(h)f
lies in
W_{K}
. Indeed, for
m\in M_{2}(K)
and
z\in K^{2}
, we have

(\sigma_{K}(h)f)(mz)=\rho_{K}(h)(f(mz))=\rho_{K}(h)(mf(z))=m(\rho_{K}(h)(f(z)))=m(\sigma_{K}(h)f)(z)

due to the assumption that
\rho_{K}(h)
preserves the
B_{K}
-action. This makes the canonical evaluation map

(*)\qquad K^{2}\otimes_{K}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{M}_{2}(K)}(K^{2},V_{K})\to V_{K}\quad{\rm given~by}~~(z,f)\mapsto f(z)

H
-equivariant.

Next we show that
W_{K}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{M}_{2}(K)}(K^{2},V_{K})
is a 2-dimensional
K
-vector space. Since
V_{K}
is a principal M
{}_{2}(K)
-module, denote it by
V_{K}={\rm M}_{2}(K)v
for some nonzero
v\in V_{K}
with the
{\rm M}_{2}(K)
-action given by left multiplication.
Write the elements in
K^{2}
as a column vector so that M
{}_{2}(K)
acts on
K^{2}
by the matrix product and
K^{2}={\rm M}_{2}(K)\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}
.
Hence an element
f\in W_{K}
is determined by
f\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}v
for some
a,b,c,d\in K
. As the stabilizer of
\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}
is the set
\left\{\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}~:~x,y\in K\right\}
, it follows that
\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}v=f\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
0&y\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)=f\left(\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}v
for all
x,y\in K
, which in turn implies
c=d=0
. Therefore
f\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}
is a
K
-linear bijection between
W_{K}
and
K^{2}
. We record this correspondence by writing
f_{(a,b)}
for
f
. This proves that
W_{K}
is a
2
-dimensional
K
-vector space on which
H
acts via
\sigma_{K}
, which is assertion (1). Notice that
f_{(a,b)}\left(\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)=\begin{pmatrix}ax&bx\\
az&bz\end{pmatrix}v
.

Observe that
K^{2}\otimes_{K}W_{K}\cong W_{K}\oplus W_{K}
. Finally we prove the surjectivity of the evaluation map
(*)
, from which the first assertion in (2) will follow. To see this, take any element
\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}v\in V_{K}
and note that

\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}v=\begin{pmatrix}x&0\\
z&0\end{pmatrix}v+\begin{pmatrix}0&y\\
0&w\end{pmatrix}v=f_{(1,0)}(\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&w\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix})+f_{(0,1)}(\begin{pmatrix}y&x\\
w&z\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}).

For the second assertion, observe that
\sigma_{K}\oplus\sigma_{K}=\sigma_{K}\otimes(1_{K}\oplus 1_{K})=\sigma_{K}\otimes\varrho_{1}(K)
as an
H\times\mathrm{GL}_{2}(K)
-module.
∎

The main applications of the above proposition are to the following settings in which
\Gamma
arises from an indefinite quaternion algebra
B
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
:

(a)

K=\mathbf{C}
and
\Gamma
cocompact and torsion-free. Denote by
A_{z}
the fiber at
z
of the universal family of 2-dimensional abelian varieties
over a modular curve
X_{\Gamma}
. Then we have a representation of

H=\pi_{1}(X_{\Gamma},z)
on
A_{z}
. It commutes with the action of the quaternions

B_{K}
on
V_{K}=H^{1}(A_{z},\mathbf{C})
. It is known that
V_{K}
is a principal
B_{K}
-module. This
gives

Corollary 1
.

Assume that
\Gamma
is cocompact and torsion-free. Then

1.

R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{C}
is, in many ways, isomorphic to a direct sum

\sigma_{\Gamma,\mathbf{C}}\oplus\sigma_{\Gamma,\mathbf{C}}
for a rank 2 local
system
\sigma_{\Gamma,\mathbf{C}}
on
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
.

2.

V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\sigma_{\Gamma,\mathbf{C}}).

(b)

Same as above, except that we consider the universal abelian variety over
X_{U}
where
U
is the compact open subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
corresponding to
\Gamma
,
z
is a point of
X_{U}
,
H
is Grothendieck’s

\pi_{1}(X_{U},\bar{z})
, the field
K
is an extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
which splits
B
, and
V_{K}=H^{1}(A_{\bar{z}},K)
.
Then
\sigma_{K}
defines a local system of
K
-vector spaces of dimension 2 on
X_{U}
for the étale topology.

(c)

For the same
K
as in (b), this time we consider
V_{K}=H^{1}(A_{\bar{z}},K)
as an
H=\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}/F)
-module
for a number field
F
over which the fiber
A_{\bar{z}}
is defined,
F\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
contains
K
, and the actions of
B_{K}
and
H
on
V_{K}
commute. This is the representation
\rho_{K}
in Proposition
2
. See more details below. An explicit description of the 2-dimensional representation
\sigma_{K}
occurring in
\rho_{K}=\sigma_{K}\oplus\sigma_{K}
for the groups
\Gamma
in Theorem
1
is provided in Proposition
5
.

Now we examine the structure of the stalks of
V^{k}(U)_{\ell}
at algebraic points in
X_{U}^{\circ}
.
To ease our notation, we shall write
V^{k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
for the stalk
(V^{k}(U)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}
and denote by
\rho^{k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
the Galois representation on this stalk.

We first study the case
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
and
U
not containing
-{\rm id}
. Let
\lambda\in X_{U}^{\circ}(L)
for a number field
L
. Since
-{\rm id}
is not in
U
,
V^{1}(U)_{\ell}
is defined as the pushforward of the sheaf
V^{1}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
on
X_{U^{\prime}}
for a neat normal open compact subgroup
U^{\prime}
of
U
via the covering map
X_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U}
. Thus the stalk of
V^{1}(U)_{\ell}
at
\lambda\in X_{U}^{\circ}(L)
is the same
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
-space as the stalk of
V^{1}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
at any point
\lambda^{\prime}\in X_{U^{\prime}}^{\circ}
above
\lambda
. Here
\lambda^{\prime}
is rational over a finite extension
L^{\prime}
of
L
. As remarked before, Ohta proved that
V^{1}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}=R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
for a family of abelian surfaces
f:A_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U^{\prime}}
. Moreover, the action of
B
on each fiber commutes with the Galois action. Hence
B
acts on
V^{1}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
and commutes with the action of a finite index subgroup
G_{L^{\prime}}
of
G_{L}
.

Let
E
be a real quadratic extension of
\mathbf{Q}
which splits
B
. Fix a prime

\mathfrak{l}
of
E
above
\ell
. Then Proposition

2
applied to
K=E_{\mathfrak{l}}
, the completion of
E
at
\mathfrak{l}
,
H=G_{L^{\prime}}
, and
\rho_{K}=\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}}E_{\mathfrak{l}}
shows that, as a
G_{L^{\prime}}
-module,
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
decomposes as

\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}}E_{\mathfrak{l}}=\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\oplus\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}=\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes(1_{G_{L^{\prime}}}\oplus 1_{G_{L^{\prime}}}).

for a canonical representation
\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{L^{\prime}}
on
E_{\mathfrak{l}}^{2}
.

We proceed to obtain a tensor product decomposition over
G_{F}
.
When
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is irreducible, using Clifford theory in the form of Theorem 2.3 of
[
48
]
we know that there are two 2-dimensional
\mathfrak{l}
-adic representations
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
and
\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{F}
, where
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
, up to a finite order character, is an extension of

\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
and
\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
has finite image, such that

(12)

\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\quad{\rm as}~G_{F}-{\rm modules}.

This also holds when
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is reducible, for in this case,
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is the sum of two degree-
2
representations of
G_{F}
whose restrictions to
G_{L^{\prime}}
are equal to
\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
. Thus they differ by a finite character
\xi
of
G_{F}
trivial on
G_{L^{\prime}}
. We may take one component to be
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
and
\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}=1\oplus\xi
.

Remark 2.
As an extension of
\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
, the representation
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
in (
12
) is unique up to twisting by a finite order character
\mu_{\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{F}
, in which case
\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
is twisted by
\mu_{\mathfrak{l}}^{-1}
.

It follows from (
12
) that
2
2

2

Assume
V
and
W
both 2-dimensional
G
-modules with a basis
\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{w_{1},w_{2}\}
respectively. Then
V\otimes W
has a basis
\{v_{i}\otimes w_{j}\}_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
so that
\bigwedge^{2}(V\otimes W)
has a basis
\{(v_{i_{1}}\otimes w_{j_{1}})\wedge(v_{i_{2}}\otimes w_{j_{2}})\mid i_{1}\neq i_{2}\text{ or }j_{1}\neq j_{2}\}
. The vectors
(v_{i}\otimes w_{1})\wedge(v_{i}\otimes w_{2})
with
i=1,2
together with
(v_{1}\otimes w_{1})\wedge(v_{2}\otimes w_{2})+(v_{2}\otimes w_{1})\wedge(v_{1}\otimes w_{2})

span a sub-
G
-module, which is isomorphic to Sym
{}^{2}V\otimes\det W
. Switching the roles of
V
and
W
will give the other one. It is independent of the choice of
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
in (
12
) by the remark above.

(13)

\bigwedge^{2}\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\left(\text{Sym}^{2}\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\text{det}\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\right)\oplus\left(\text{det}\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\right)

as a sum of two degree-3
G_{F}
-modules. In particular,
\left(\text{Sym}^{2}\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\text{det}\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\right)|_{G_{L}^{\prime}}
is a subrepresentation of
\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}.

By Proposition
2
(2),
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is also a
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{l}})
-module. To see the
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{l}})
-module structure of
\bigwedge^{2}\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
, we regard (
13
) as
G_{L^{\prime}}
-modules. As such, on the right hand side, the first factor is
\text{Sym}^{2}(\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\mid G_{L^{\prime}})\otimes 1_{E_{\mathfrak{l}}}
, which consists of three copies of a 1-dimensional
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{l}})
-module, while the second factor reads
\det(\sigma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\mid G_{L^{\prime}})\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}(\varrho_{1}(E_{\mathfrak{l}}))
, which is a degree-3 irreducible
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{l}})
-module.

On the other hand, by Proposition (4.3.1) and the proof of Proposition (4.4.2) of
[
55
]
,

\bigwedge^{2}\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}=\bigwedge^{2}\rho^{1}(U^{\prime})_{\lambda^{\prime},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}\cong(R^{2}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda^{\prime}}}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}\cong(V^{2}(U^{\prime})_{\bar{\lambda^{\prime}},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}})\oplus E_{\mathfrak{l}}(-1)^{3}

as
G_{L^{\prime}}
-modules,
where
E_{\mathfrak{l}}(-1)
denotes the Tate twist of the constant sheaf
E_{\mathfrak{l}}
by
-1
. As
\mathrm{GL}_{2}(E_{\mathfrak{l}})
-modules, on the right side, the first factor is of degree-3 irreducible generically, while the second factor is three copies of a 1-dimensional module. This shows that
V^{2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
and
\text{Sym}^{2}\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\text{det}\gamma_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
agree as
G_{L^{\prime}}
-modules, and hence as
G_{F}
-modules, they agree up to a (finite order) character of
G_{F}
trivial on
G_{L^{\prime}}
. Thus there is a finite order character
\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{F}
such that the representation
\rho^{2}_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{F}
on
V^{2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is

(14)

\displaystyle\rho^{2}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}=\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes\text{Sym}^{2}\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}.

We show that this is a general phenomenon.

Proposition 3
.

Let
U
be a group generated by an open compact subgroup of
G(\mathbb{A}_{f})
and a subset of the Atkin-Lehner operators. Suppose
-{\rm id}\notin U
. Let
\lambda
be an algebraic point of
X_{U}^{\circ}
.

(1) Suppose
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
for an indefinite quaternion algebra
B
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.
Let
E
be a real quadratic field that splits
B
. Fix a prime
\ell
and a prime
\mathfrak{l}
of
E
above
\ell
.
Suppose the 4-dimensional
\ell
-adic representation
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}

on the stalk
V^{1}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
is a
G_{F}
-module for some number field
F
.
Then there is a
2
-dimensional

\mathfrak{l}
-adic representation
\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
and a finite order character
\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
of
G_{F}
such that for all integers
k\geq 1
, the representation
\rho^{2k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}}E_{\mathfrak{l}}
on the fiber
V^{2k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}}E_{\mathfrak{l}}
is given by

(15)

\displaystyle\rho^{2k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}}E_{\mathfrak{l}}=\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}^{k}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2k}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})

as
G_{F}
-modules. Moreover,
\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\det(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})=\epsilon_{\mathfrak{l}}
, where
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{l}}
denotes the
\mathfrak{l}
-adic cyclotomic character.

(2) Suppose
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
.
Then for all integers
k\geq 1
,

(16)

\displaystyle\rho^{k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}=\mathrm{Sym}^{k}(\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}),

and
\det\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
is the cyclotomic character
\epsilon_{\ell}
.

In both cases, the determinant of the representation is the cyclotomic character raised to the degree of the representation.

Proof.

Let
\rho
be a degree-2 characteristic zero representation of a group. We have, for
m\geq 4
,

(17)

\displaystyle\qquad{\rm Sym}^{m-2}(\rho)\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2}(\rho)\cong{\rm Sym}^{m}(\rho)\oplus(\det(\rho)\otimes{\rm Sym}^{m-2}(\rho))\oplus(\det(\rho)^{2}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{m-4}(\rho))

by checking the characters of the representations or the Clebsch Gordan coefficient formula.

Case (1)
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
. Applying (
17
) to the representation
\rho=\varrho_{1}(E)
of GL
{}_{2}(E)
, we obtain the canonical decomposition

\varrho_{m-2}(E)\otimes\varrho_{2}(E)\cong\varrho_{m}(E)\oplus(\det\varrho_{1}(E)\otimes\varrho_{m-2}(E))\oplus((\det\varrho_{1}(E))^{2}\otimes\varrho_{m-4}(E)).

As discussed in §
2.2.2
, the representations
\varrho^{\prime}_{m}(\mathbf{Q})
of
B^{\times}
for
m\geq 2
even and
m=1
may be regarded as the restriction of
\varrho_{m}(E)
to
B^{\times}
under the identification
B^{\times}=\theta(B^{\times})\subset{\rm GL}_{2}(E)
. Thus the above identity holds with
\varrho^{\prime}_{j}(E)
replacing
\varrho_{j}(\mathbf{Q})
, which in turn gives rise to the following identity on sheaves

(18)

\displaystyle V^{2k-2}(U)_{\ell}\otimes V^{2}(U)_{\ell}\cong V^{2k}(U)_{\ell}\oplus(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(-1)\otimes V^{2k-2}(U)_{\ell})\oplus(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}(-2)\otimes V^{2k-4}(U)_{\ell})

by the proof of Proposition (4.4.2) in
[
55
]
since the
\ell
-adic rank-1 sheaf arising from
\det(\varrho^{\prime}_{1}(\mathbf{Q}))
is the Tate twist by
-1
of the constant sheaf
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
on which the Galois group acts by the cyclotomic character
\epsilon_{\ell}
. Also,
V^{0}(U)_{\ell}
is the constant sheaf
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
on
X_{U}
. The same holds when each sheaf is tensored with
E_{\mathfrak{l}}
.

We now prove (
15
) by induction on
k
. When
k=1
, this is (
14
) proved above. Assume the statement holds for
2k-2
, that is,
as
G_{L}
-modules,

V^{2k-2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}\cong\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}^{k-1}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2k-2}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}).

The two surjective
G_{F}
-homomorphisms

(V^{2k-2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}})\otimes(V^{2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}})\to V^{2k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}

from (
18
) tensored with
E_{\mathfrak{l}}
and

(\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}^{k-1}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2k-2}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}))\otimes(\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}))\to\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}^{k}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2k}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})

from (
17
) with
\rho=\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}
and
m=2k
both arise from the symmetrization map. As the left hand sides are isomorphic
G_{F}
-modules by induction hypothesis, so are the right hand sides. This proves (
15
).

Similarly, replacing the right hand sides of the above two surjective homomorphisms by the middle term from the right side of (
18
) and (
17
) respectively, we obtain two isomorphic
G_{F}
-modules
\det(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})\otimes\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}^{k}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2k-2}(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})
and
E_{\mathfrak{l}}(-1)\otimes V^{2k-2}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\otimes E_{\mathfrak{l}}
, which implies the identity
\chi_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}}\det(\eta_{\lambda,\mathfrak{l}})=\epsilon_{\mathfrak{l}}
since the
G_{F}
action on
E_{\mathfrak{l}}(-1)
is
\epsilon_{\mathfrak{l}}
.

Case (2)
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
. This follows from Deligne
[
21
]
.
∎

Remark 3.
For the groups
\Gamma
considered in this paper, the curves
X_{\Gamma}
are defined over
\mathbf{Q}
, hence
X_{\Gamma}=X_{U}
for the group
U
corresponding to
\Gamma
. Formulae (
15
) and (
16
) indicate that, when
-{\rm id}\notin U
, the Frobenius traces of
\rho^{2k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
and
\rho^{k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
can be computed from those of
\rho^{2}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
and
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
, using the relation given by (
2
) and (
7
), respectively. When
U

contains
-{\rm id}
, by definition,
\rho^{2k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
is an extension of
\rho^{2k}(U^{\prime})_{\lambda^{\prime},\ell}=\chi_{\lambda^{\prime},\ell}^{k}\otimes{\rm Sym}^{2k}(\eta_{\lambda^{\prime},\ell})
for some neat compact open normal subgroup
U^{\prime}
of
U
, resulting from the sheaf
V^{2k}(U)_{\ell}
being a push-forward of
V^{2k}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
. While
\rho^{2}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
may no longer be a symmetric square up to twist by a character, the recursive relation (
18
) holds over
X_{U}^{\circ}
. In particular, this implies that if
\lambda
is in
X_{U}^{\circ}(\mathbf{Q})
, then at a good prime
p
, the trace of
\rho^{2k}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
at Frob
p
is equal to
F_{k}(S,T)
defined by (
2
) with
S
being the trace of
\rho^{2}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}({\rm Frob}_{p})
and
T=p
, the same expression as the case
U
not containing
-{\rm id}
.
This will be used in the proofs of the theorems listed in §
1.1
. Thus our work is reduced to determining the Frobenius traces of
\rho^{2}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
or
\rho^{1}(U)_{\lambda,\ell}
as appropriate. A key contribution of this paper is to showcase certain groups
U
(or their corresponding
\Gamma
as discussed in §
1.1
) for which we can identify suitable hypergeometric sheaves with explicit Galois actions which are isomorphic to the automorphic sheaves in question.

We end this section by discussing the integrality of Frobenius traces of the Galois action on the stalk
V^{k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
at an algebraic point
\lambda
in
X_{U}^{\circ}
. First assume the group
G=\textsf{B}^{\times}
for a definite quaternion division algebra
B
defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.
If
A
is a 2-dimensional abelian variety defined over a number field
L
with multiplication by
B
, then the action of
\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{L}/L)
on the first étale cohomology
group factors as

\rho_{\ell}:\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{L}/L)\to(B\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})^{\times}={\textsf{B}}^{\times}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\subset\mathrm{Aut}(H^{1}(A\otimes_{L}\bar{L},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))\sim\mathrm{GL}_{4}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}).

This is because the commutant of
B
in

\mathrm{End}(H^{1}(A\otimes_{L}\bar{L},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))\simeq\mathrm{M}_{4}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
is

B_{\ell}=B\otimes_{\mathbf{Q}}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
. It follows from the construction of the
\ell
-adic sheaf in §
2.2.2
that,
the stalks

V^{k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
in an
L
-rational point
\lambda\in X_{U}^{\circ}(L)
, as a Galois module, are in the shape

\varrho_{k}^{\prime}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\circ\rho_{\ell}:\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{L}/L)\to{\textsf{B}}^{\times}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})\to\textsf{ GL}_{m}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})

where
m=k+1
for
k
even and
m=2(k+1)
for
k
odd, as explained in §
2.2.2
.
From this, one can prove that the coefficients of the characteristic
polynomials in unramified primes
\mathfrak{p}
of
L

(19)

\displaystyle\det\left(1-T\,\mathrm{Frob}_{\mathfrak{p}}\mid V^{k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}\right)\in\mathbf{Z}[T]

have integer coefficients. We will not prove this here. The above reasoning assumes that

U
is neat. To do a general
U
,
which may include some Atkin-Lehner operators as above, note that
the Frobenius traces can be computed, as in the theory of Artin
L
-functions, by averaging
the Frobenius traces in the fibers over
\lambda
in a covering

X_{U^{\prime}}\to X_{U}
for a neat
U^{\prime}
.

A similar argument yields the same conclusion for
G=\textsf{GL}_{2}
. In this case
A
is an elliptic curve,
\mathrm{End}(H^{1}(A\otimes_{L}\bar{L},\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}))\simeq\mathrm{M}_{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
and the Galois action on the stalk
V^{k}(U)_{\bar{\lambda},\ell}
has the shape
\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{L}/L)\to\textsf{GL}_{k+1}(\mathbf{Q}_{\ell})
.

3.
Groups

3.1.
Hypergeometric functions over
\mathbf{C}

We first recall some relevant notation in hypergeometric functions. Let
\Gamma(x)
denote the Gamma function. For
k\in\mathbf{Z}
and
a\in\mathbf{C}
, define the Pochhammer symbol
(a)_{k}:=\Gamma(a+k)/\Gamma(a)=a(a+1)\cdots(a+k-1)
.
A hypergeometric datum is a pair of multi-sets
\alpha=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\},\beta=\{b_{1}=1,b_{2},\cdots,b_{n}\}
with
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbf{Q}
to which we associate the Hypergeometric function in variable
t\in\mathbf{C}
:

(20)

\displaystyle F(\alpha,\beta;t)=\,_{n}F_{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}a_{1}&a_{2}&\cdots&a_{n}\\
&b_{2}&\cdots&b_{n}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]:=\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{(a_{1})_{k}\cdots(a_{n})_{k}}{(b_{1})_{k}\cdots(b_{n})_{k}}t^{k}.

It satisfies the Fuchsian ordinary differential equation below with three regular singular points at
0,1,\infty
:

\left[\theta\left(\theta+b_{2}-1\right)\cdots\left(\theta+b_{n}-1\right)-t\left(\theta+a_{1}\right)\cdots\left(\theta+a_{n}\right)\right]F=0,\quad\text{where}~\theta:=t\frac{d}{dt}.

The local solutions of this equation form a rank-
n
hypergeometric local system.

Theorem 4
.

The local exponents of the above hypergeometric differential equation are

(21)

\begin{split}0,1-b_{2},\cdots,1-b_{n}&\quad\text{ at }t=0,\\
a_{1},\,a_{2},\,\cdots,\,a_{n}&\quad\text{ at }t=\infty,\\
0,1,2,\cdots,n-2,\gamma&\quad\text{ at }t=1,\end{split}

respectively, where

(22)

\gamma=-1+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}.

See
[
15
, §2]
by Beukers and Heckman for more details. We say that a local monodromy matrix in
\rm{GL}_{n}(\mathbf{R})
has exponents
(r_{1},\cdots,r_{n})\in(\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z})^{n}
if its eigenvalues are
e^{2\pi ir_{1}},\cdots,e^{2\pi ir_{n}}
.
In particular, the above theorem
says the local monodromy at 1 with monodromy matrix
M_{1}
is a pseudoreflection (cf.
[
15
,
40
]
), namely the rank of
M_{1}-I_{n}
is 1, where
I_{n}
stands for the rank-
n
identity matrix.

Next we recall a couple useful transformation formulae. Combined with the Euler transformation formula (cf.
[
6
, Theorem 2.2.5]
)

(23)

\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}a&b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&c\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]=(1-t)^{c-a-b}\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}c-a&c-b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&c\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right],

a version of the Clausen formula (
[
6
]
or
[
49
, Eqn. (39)]
) says that for
a,b,t\in\mathbf{C}
,

(24)

{(1-t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}}\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&a-b+\frac{1}{2}&b-a+\frac{1}{2}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&a+b+\frac{1}{2}&\frac{3}{2}-a-b\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]=\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}a&b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&a+b+\frac{1}{2}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}1-a&1-b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\frac{3}{2}-a-b\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]

when both sides are convergent.

3.2.
A motivating example

We demonstrate the main motivation of this section using the following classical example. Let
\Gamma=\mathrm{PSL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})\cong(2,3,\infty)
whose modular curve
X_{\Gamma}=X(1)
is parameterized by the modular
j
-function. The open subset
X(1)^{\circ}:=X(1)\setminus\{0,1728,\infty\}
admits the universal elliptic curve

(25)

\mathcal{E}_{j}:\quad y^{2}+xy=x^{3}-\frac{36x+1}{j-1728}.

Through the fiber map
f:\mathcal{E}_{j}\mapsto j
, the local constant sheaf
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
on
\mathcal{E}_{j}
gives rise to

\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=R^{1}f_{*}\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
on
X(1)^{\circ}
. The Picard-Fuchs equation of this elliptic fibration, using an algorithm in
[
66
, Theorem 1.5]
by Stienstra and Beukers, is hypergeometric with one solution given by
(1-t)^{-1/4}\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{12}&\frac{5}{12}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]
with
t=1728/j
. Note that the hypergeometric datum
\{\{\frac{1}{12},\frac{5}{12}\},\{1,1\}\}
is not defined over
\mathbf{Q}
. However

\left((1-t)^{-1/4}\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{12}&\frac{5}{12}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]\right)^{2}\overset{\eqref{eq:Euler}}{=}\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{12}&\frac{5}{12}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{11}{12}&\frac{7}{12}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]\\
\overset{\eqref{eq:clausen1}}{=}(1-t)^{-1/2}\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{6}&\frac{5}{6}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right],

in which the hypergeometric datum
\{\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}\},\{1,1,1\}\}
in the last expression is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
.

Here a datum
HD=\{\alpha=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\},\beta=\{b_{1}=1,b_{2},\cdots,b_{n}\}\}
with
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
is said to be defined over
\mathbf{Q}
if the set of column vectors
\{\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\\
b_{1}\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}a_{n}\\
b_{n}\end{pmatrix}\}
mod
\mathbf{Z}
is invariant under multiplication by all
r\in(\mathbf{Z}/M\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
, where
M=M(HD)
, called the level of
HD
, is the least positive common denominators of
a_{1},...,a_{n},b_{1},...,b_{n}
.
See Definition 1 in
[
49
, §2.2]
. Also, in this paper we mainly consider primitive hypergeometric data
HD
, namely
a_{i}-b_{j}\notin\mathbf{Z}
for all
i,j
, such that the corresponding local systems are irreducible.

3.3.
The groups to be considered

We now restrict our discussion from congruence subgroups of
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
to a class of arithmetic triangle groups which have been classified by Takeuchi
[
67
,
68
]
. Petersson
[
59
]
(see also
[
19
]
by Clark and Voight) provides an algorithm to express the generators as matrices over a totally real subfield of a cyclotomic field.
Among all arithmetic triangle groups, we
consider those
whose corresponding rank-2 local systems
V
are hypergeometric with datum
\alpha_{2}=\{a,b\},\beta_{2}=\{1,c\}
such that either

a) The datum
\{\alpha_{2},\beta_{2}\}
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
; or,

b) Sym
{}^{2}V
is also hypergeometric, which is very special due to the pseudoreflection requirement, and its corresponding datum is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
(as the example in §
3.2
).

The above criteria give rise to the following groups, classified according to the conditions they satisfy:

a.I)
(m,\infty,\infty)
with
m=3,\infty
,

b.I)
(2,m,\infty)
with
m=3,4,6,\infty
,

b.II)
(2,4,6),(2,6,6)
.

All groups are isomorphic to a subgroup of SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
mod
\pm I_{2}
, while those in a.I) are the only ones isomorphic to a subgroup of SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
(in fact
\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
) not containing
-I_{2}
.

We now describe how we arrive at the lists.
Given a triangle group
\Gamma=(e_{0},e_{1},e_{\infty})
, following Theorem 9 of
[
72
]
by Y. Yang we introduce
the following hypergeometric parameters:

a=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{e_{1}}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}-\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}),\quad b=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{e_{1}}-\frac{1}{e_{0}}+\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}),\quad c=1-\frac{1}{e_{0}},

and

\tilde{a}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{e_{1}}+\frac{1}{e_{0}}-\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}),\quad\tilde{b}=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{e_{1}}+\frac{1}{e_{0}}+\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}),\quad\tilde{c}=1+\frac{1}{e_{0}}.

Using these Yang wrote down an explicit basis for
S_{k}(\Gamma)
in terms of
\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}a&b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&c\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]
and
\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\tilde{a}&\tilde{b}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\tilde{c}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]
which satisfy ordinary differential equations that are projectively equivalent. When
e_{0}=e_{1}=\infty
, the corresponding arithmetic triangle group
(e_{\infty},\infty,\infty)
can be identified with an index-2 subgroup of a Hecke group up to conjugation in PSL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
. We further check whether they have a realization in SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
not containing
-I_{2}
. This process leads to the list a.I) above with
HD(\Gamma)=\{\alpha=\{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}),\frac{1}{2}(1+\frac{1}{e_{\infty}})\},\beta=\{1,1\}\}
, in which the Hauptmodul is chosen such that the two cusps are located at
t=0
and
t=1
.

If, in particular,
e_{1}=2
, then
the Clausen formula recalled in (
24
) implies

(26)

\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}a&b\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&c\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\tilde{a}&\tilde{b}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\tilde{c}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]=\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1-\frac{1}{e_{0}}&1+\frac{1}{e_{0}}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right].

The hypergeometric datum corresponding to the right hand side of (
26
) is

(27)

\displaystyle HD:=HD(\Gamma):=\left\{\alpha=\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{e_{\infty}},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{e_{\infty}}\},\beta=\{1,1-\frac{1}{e_{0}},1+\frac{1}{e_{0}}\}\right\}

in which
\alpha
and
\beta
are self-dual.
The datum is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
if
e_{0},e_{\infty}\in\{3,4,6,\infty\}
. This leads to the groups listed in b.I) for
\Gamma
non-cocompact and b.II) for
\Gamma
cocompact.

As explained in §
3.1
, a hypergeometric sheaf is defined on
\mathbf{P}^{1}
with singularities at
0,1,\infty
. When we associate a hypergeometric sheaf to a triangle group
\Gamma
, the three singularities of the sheaf are set at the three vertices of the curve
X_{\Gamma}
(as a compact Riemann surface). For this to happen, the model for
X_{\Gamma}
should be a genus zero curve with the three singular points that are
\mathbf{Q}
-rational.

The models we choose for the non-cocompact groups are as follows

\begin{array}[]{cccc}(2,\infty,\infty)&(2,3,\infty)&(2,4,\infty)&(2,6,\infty)\\
\Gamma_{0}(2)/\{\pm I\}&{\rm PSL}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})&\langle\Gamma_{0}(2),w_{2}\rangle/\{\pm I\}&\langle\Gamma_{0}(3),w_{3}\rangle/\{\pm I\}\end{array}

where
w_{2}
and
w_{3}
are Atkin-Lehner involutions, so that
X_{\Gamma}
are projective curves over
\mathbf{Q}
and all elliptic points and cusps are
\mathbf{Q}
-rational. Hence we may choose
\lambda(\Gamma)
as described in the table in Theorem
1
. For the cocompact group
(2,4,6)
, more explanation is needed.
The quotient of the norm-
1
group of
O^{1}_{B_{6}}
by its center
\pm 1
gives the group
(2,2,3,3)
in the class II group
1(b)
. It is normalized by the Atkin-Lehner involutions
w_{2}
,
w_{3}
, and
w_{6}=w_{2}w_{3}
. The extended groups
\langle(2,2,3,3),w_{i}\rangle
for
i=3,2,6
respectively are the groups
(2,6,6)
,
(3,4,4)
and
(2,2,2,3)
, and
(2,4,6)=\langle(2,2,3,3),w_{2},w_{3}\rangle
. The curve

X_{(2,2,3,3)}
is a genus 0 curve defined over
\mathbf{Q}
with the canonical model given by
x^{2}+3y^{2}+z^{2}=0
(cf.
[
45
]
). As described in Baba-Granath
[
10
]
, it is a 2-fold cover of the curves
X_{(2,6,6)}
,
X_{(3,4,4)}
,
X_{(2,2,2,3)}
, all projective lines over
\mathbf{Q}
, via the covering maps sending
[x:y:z]\in X_{(2,2,3,3)}
to
[y:z]
,
[x:z]
,
[x:y]
, respectively. The curve
X_{(2,4,6)}
is also a projective line over
\mathbf{Q}
; it is covered by
X_{(2,6,6)}
,
X_{(3,4,4)}
,
X_{(2,2,2,3)}
with respective covering maps

(28)

\pi_{3}:[y:z]\mapsto\left[-3y^{2}-z^{2}:y^{2}\right],\quad\pi_{2}:[x:z]\mapsto\left[x^{2}:\frac{-x^{2}-z^{2}}{3}\right],\quad\pi_{6}:[x:y]\mapsto\left[x^{2}:y^{2}\right].

These are the models we choose. As such, the three elliptic points on
X_{(2,4,6)}
are
[0:1],[1:0],[-3:1]
, all
\mathbf{Q}
-rational; for the sake of convenience we may choose the Hauptmodul
\lambda
for the curve
X_{(2,4,6)}
as given in Theorem
1
.
But
X_{(2,6,6)}
,
X_{(3,4,4)}
and
X_{(2,2,2,3)}
all have elliptic points rational only over a quadratic extension of
\mathbf{Q}
.
For this reason, we did not include
(2,6,6)
in Theorem
1
. Since
X_{(2,6,6)}
is a 2-fold cover of
X_{(2,4,6)}
with an explicit covering map, we will use the pullback of the hypergeometric sheaf on
X_{(2,4,6)}
to deal with (2,6,6), see Theorem
14
in Section
7.1
.

The rank-2 hypergeometric local systems
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}
for groups in a.I) are listed in the Table
2
below.

.

.

, in which branches for

.
