3.4.
Isomorphisms using rigidity

For arithmetic triangle groups, the rigidity theorems may be applied
to compare the automorphic sheaves described in §
2
and hypergeometric local systems arising from local solutions of hypergeometric differential equations. More details are discussed in
[
40
]
, where
\ell
-adic hypergeometric sheaves on the algebraic group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
are also introduced.

Theorem 5
.

(Katz
[
40
, Rigidity Theorem 3.5.4]
)
Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be two irreducible local systems on
(\mathbb{G}_{m}-\{s_{0}\})^{\mbox{an}}
of the same rank
n\geq 1
, where
s_{0}
is a fixed nonzero value, and satisfying

1)

The local monodromies at
s_{0}
of both
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are pseudoreflections;

2)

At both 0 and
\infty
,
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
have the same characteristic polynomials of local monodromies respectively.

Then
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
are isomorphic.

For the groups in a) list, the generator
T
listed in Table
2
is a pseudoreflection. From Theorems
4
and
5
, and the information in Table
2
, we have the following conclusion.

Theorem 6
.

The local system
V^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}=\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}

given in the last column of Table
2
.

We now illustrate how to use Katz’s rigidity theorem to relate
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}

and hypergeometric local system directly for b) groups (which also works for
(2,6,6)
). For both local systems,
s_{0}=1
.

Theorem 7
.

For each group
\Gamma
in Table
3
, let
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
be the rank-3 sheaf as in §
2.1
. Then
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}{=\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}}
, where
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
are the hypergeometric sheaves in Table
3
. Moreover
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is isomorphic to the symmetric square of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
listed in the second to the last column in Table
3
.

Proof.

By Clausen formula,
\rm{Sym}^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
. It remains to prove the first statement.

The groups
\Gamma=(2,m,\infty)
in b.I) with
m=3
,
4
,
6
are isomorphic to the projective groups PSL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
,
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(2)/\{\pm I_{2}\}
, and
\Gamma_{0}^{+}(3)/\{\pm I_{2}\}
respectively. They are generated by the matrices

T:=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad S:=\begin{pmatrix}0&1/\sqrt{a}\\
-\sqrt{a}&0\end{pmatrix},\quad(ST)^{-1},\text{ where }\,a=\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\rfloor.

Thus the fixed points of
(ST)^{-1}
are

\frac{-1+\sqrt{-3}}{2}
,
\frac{-1+\sqrt{-1}}{2}
,
\frac{-1+\sqrt{-1/3}}{2}
respectively. Also

\mathrm{Sym}^{2}T=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&1\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathrm{Sym}^{2}S=\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1/a\\
0&-1&0\\
a&0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(ST)^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}a&2&1/a\\
-a&-1&0\\
a&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

We choose a Hauptmodul
\lambda
for
\Gamma
(such as
\lambda=1728/j
for PSL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{Z})
) whose values at the singular points are as described in Theorem
1
. Precisely, it has a simple zero at infinity, takes value 1 at
the fixed point of
S
, and has a simple pole at that of
ST
. The sheaf
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is defined in §
2.1
. While
V^{1}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
is not defined because the lift of
\Gamma
in SL
{}_{2}(\mathbf{R})
contains
-I_{2}
, nonetheless the above three symmetric square matrices can be used to describe
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
. Indeed
the Jordan normal forms of the local monodromy matrices of
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
are

\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&\infty\\
\hline\cr\begin{pmatrix}1&&\\
&1&\\
&&-1\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\
0&1&1\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}-1&&\\
&-\zeta_{m}&\\
&&-\zeta_{m}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\end{array}

In particular, the monodromy at 1 is a pseudoreflection. The monodromy matrices at
1,0,\infty
have characteristic polynomials
(T-1)^{2}(T+1),(T-1)^{3},(T+1)(T+\zeta_{m})(T+\zeta_{m}^{-1})
respectively, which coincide with
those of the hypergeometric local system
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}
with datum
HD(\Gamma)
(see Theorem
4
). We conclude from Theorem
5
that they are isomorphic.

The same argument applies to the fourth group
\Gamma=(2,\infty,\infty)
in b.I), which is isomorphic to
\Gamma_{0}(2)/\{\pm I_{2}\}
. The only difference is the generator
S=\begin{pmatrix}-1&1\\
-2&1\end{pmatrix}
so that
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}S
has eigenvalues
-1,1,-1
and
\mathrm{Sym}^{2}(ST)^{-1}
has eigenvalues
1,1,1
.

The remaining case can be verified in the like manner. From the exponents of the generators for (2,4,6) we know the exponents of their symmetric squares are
\{\frac{1}{4},\,1,\,\frac{3}{4}\}
,
\{\frac{7}{6},\,1,\,\frac{5}{6}\}
, and
\{\frac{1}{2},\,\frac{1}{2},1\}
respectively, which coincide with the local exponents of the order-3 differential equation satisfied by
(1-t)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{4}&\frac{3}{4}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\frac{7}{6}&\frac{5}{6}\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right].

∎

3.5.
Comparison Theorem

The results of this section appear in
[
36
]
. We include them here for the
convenience of the reader.
Let
F
be a number field and
R=O_{F}[1/N]
the localization of the ring of
integers of
F
for an integer
N\geq 1
. Let
S=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
. We let

\eta=\mathrm{Spec}(F)
, the generic point of
S
, and
\bar{\eta}=\mathrm{Spec}(\bar{F})
for an
algebraic closure of
F
. Let
X/S
be an irreducible separated scheme, smooth and of finite type over
S
,
with geometrically connected fibers.
We can choose a geometric generic point
\bar{\xi}:\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{F(X)})\to X
which
lies over
\bar{\eta}
, where
F(X)
is the function field of
X
. We let
X_{\eta}
and
X_{\bar{\eta}}

be the schemes over
\mathrm{Spec}{F}
and
\mathrm{Spec}{\bar{F}}
obtained from
X
by base-change.

We consider two geometrically irreducible lisse
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-
sheaves (for the étale topology)
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{G}
on
X
. These are equivalent to
two
\ell
-adic representations

\rho_{\mathscr{F}}:\pi_{1}(X,\bar{\xi})\longrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(V),\quad\rho_{\mathscr{G}}:\pi_{1}(X,\bar{\xi})\longrightarrow\mathrm{GL}(W)

for finite-dimensional
\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
-vector spaces
V
,
W
. Geometrically irreducible means:
the restrictions

\rho_{\mathscr{F}}\mid X_{\bar{\eta}},\ \ \rho_{\mathscr{G}}\mid X_{\bar{\eta}}

of
\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})
are irreducible. Note that we have
a surjective homomorphism

\pi_{1}(X_{\eta},\bar{\xi})\to\pi_{1}(X,\bar{\xi})

and an exact sequence

0\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})\longrightarrow\pi_{1}(X_{\eta},\bar{\xi})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{F}/F)\longrightarrow 0.

Now let
\varphi:R\to\mathbf{C}
be an embedding. We obtain a scheme

X_{\varphi,\mathbf{C}}
over
\mathbf{C}
. This also defines an analytic space

X_{\varphi}^{\mathrm{an}}:=X_{\varphi}(\mathbf{C})
. Since
\varphi
will be fixed,
we will drop it from the notation. There is a canonical map

\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},u)\to\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbf{C}},u)

(left-hand side: topological fundamental group; right-hand side, the étale fundamental group)
which identifies the right-hand side with the profinite completion of left-hand side (Riemann’s existence
theorem). In particular, this map has dense image. Here we can take

u
to be the geometric
u:\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C})\to X

\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{C})\to\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{F(X)})=\bar{\xi}\to X

where the first arrow is induced by some embedding
\bar{\varphi}:\overline{F(X)}\to\mathbf{C}
which extends
\varphi
.
It is known that there are isomorphisms
\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbf{C}},u)=\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},u)=\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})
induced
by
\bar{\varphi}
. The first holds because
F
has characteristic 0; the second is a change in base-point.
Choose an isomorphism
\iota:\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}\cong\mathbf{C}
. The theorem that follows will not depend
on this artificial choice.

Composing all these, we get representations

\rho^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathscr{F}}:\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},u)\to\pi_{1}(X_{\mathbf{C}},u)=\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})\overset{\rho_{\mathscr{F}}}{\longrightarrow}\mathrm{GL}(V)\cong\mathrm{GL}(V_{\mathbf{C}})

where
V_{\mathbf{C}}=V\otimes_{\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell},\iota}\mathbf{C}
. We get a similar story for
\rho^{\mathrm{an}}_{\mathscr{G}}
.
We let
{\sf F}
and
{\sf G}
be the
\mathbf{C}
-local systems on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
that arise from these
representations of the fundamental group. Also
\mathcal{D}({\sf F})
and
\mathcal{D}({\sf G})

the regular holonomic
\mathcal{D}
-modules (=connections with regular singular points) corresponding
to these by the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence.

Theorem 8
.

Under these assumptions (and
\mathscr{F}
,
\mathscr{G}
geometrically irreducible), if
the local systems
{\sf F}
and
{\sf G}
on
X^{\mathrm{an}}
are isomorphic (equivalently
if the
\mathcal{D}
-modules
\mathcal{D}({\sf F})
and
\mathcal{D}({\sf G})
are isomorphic),
then there is a continuous character
\chi:\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{F}/F)\to\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
, such that

\mathscr{G}_{\eta}\cong\mathscr{F}_{\eta}\otimes\chi.

Proof.

There is a matrix
M:V_{\mathbf{C}}\to W_{\mathbf{C}}
that intertwines
the representation of
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},u)
given by
{\sf F}
and
{\sf G}
. Then

\iota^{-1}(M):V\to W
is a matrix that intertwines the representations of
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},u)
in

\mathrm{GL}(V)
and
\mathrm{GL}(W)
.
But
\pi_{1}(X^{\mathrm{an}},u)
has dense image in
\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})
and the
representations given by
\mathscr{G}
and
\mathscr{F}
on
V
,
W
are continuous. Thus
by continuity
\iota^{-1}(M)
will intertwine those representations. Therefore the representations

\rho_{\mathscr{F}}\mid X_{\bar{\eta}},\ \ \rho_{\mathscr{G}}\mid X_{\bar{\eta}}

of
\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\eta})
are isomorphic. From the exact sequence above, and
the fact that these representations are isomorphic we get, by the lemma below, a character

\chi:\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{F}/F)\to\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}^{\times}
and an isomorphism

(\rho_{\mathscr{G}}\mid X_{\eta})=(\rho_{\mathscr{F}}\mid X_{\eta})\otimes\chi

as representations of
\pi_{1}(X_{\eta},\bar{\xi})
.

∎

The following is well-known.

Lemma 2
.

Given an exact sequence of groups

\begin{diagram}

and two finite-dimensional representations
\rho:G\to\mathrm{GL}(V)
and

\sigma:G\to\mathrm{GL}(W)
where
V,W
are vector spaces over an
algebraically closed field
k
.
Suppose that
\rho\mid H
and
\sigma\mid H
are irreducible and isomorphic.
Then there is a character
\chi:G/H\to\mathrm{GL}_{1}(k)=k^{\times}
, such that

\sigma\cong\rho\otimes\chi:=\rho\otimes(\chi\circ b)
. If
k
is a topological field and
\rho,\sigma
are continuous
representations, then
\chi
is a continuous character.

Proof.

Let
Z=\mathrm{Hom}_{k}(V,W)
. This is a finite-dimensional
k
-space with a
G
-action:

(g\cdot f)(v):=\sigma(g)f(\rho(g)^{-1}v),\text{ where }v\in V,f\in Z,g\in G.

A fixed vector
f
, namely
g\cdot f=f,\forall g\in G
, is a
G
-equivariant map
f:V\to W
. By hypothesis
there is an
H
-fixed vector corresponding to an
H
-equivariant isomorphism

f:V\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}W
. Since these
H
-spaces are irreducible, any nonzero

H
-equivariant map
f:V\to W
is an isomorphsm, and any two such differ by a scalar
multiple (Schur’s lemma). Thus,
Z^{H}=k\cdot f
. The
G
-action on
Z
induces
a
G/H
-action on
Z^{H}
. Since this is one-dimensional, this is a character
\chi
which is defined
by
g\cdot f=\chi(g)f
for all
g\in G
. One checks that
f:V\to W
is an isomorphism
which is equivariant for the
\rho\otimes\chi
action on
V
and
\sigma
on
W
.
∎

We can give a stronger version of Theorem
8
if we assume in addition that
X/S
has a section, and that

X/S
is the complement in
Z/S
of a divisor with normal crossings
D/S
, where
Z/S
is proper
and smooth. We also assume that
\ell
is invertible on
X
and
S
. Under those assumptions, then we have an exact sequence
(
[
1
, Ch. XIII, Prop. 4.3, and Examples 4.4]
)

\begin{diagram}.

Here
\mathbb{L}
is a
set of primes invertible on
S
, namely coprime to
N
.
\pi_{1}^{\mathbb{L}}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})

is the pro-
\mathbb{L}
-quotient of
\pi_{1}(X_{\bar{\eta}},\bar{\xi})
. If
K
is the kernel of the canonical homomorphism

\pi_{1}(X,\bar{\xi})\to\pi_{1}(S,\bar{\eta})
and
N\subset K
is the smallest normal subgroup such that

K/N
is a pro-
\mathbb{L}
-group, then
N\subset\pi_{1}(X,\bar{\xi})
is a normal subgroup, and
we denote
\pi_{1}^{\prime}(X,\bar{\xi})=\pi_{1}^{\prime}(X,\bar{\xi})/N
. If we assume that the representations

\rho_{\mathscr{F}}
and
\rho_{\mathscr{G}}
factor through
\pi_{1}^{\prime}(X,\bar{\xi})
and are
geometrically irreducible, we can conclude that there exists a continuous character

\chi:\pi_{1}(S,\bar{\eta})\to\bar{\mathbf{Q}}^{\times}_{\ell}
such that
\mathscr{G}\cong\mathscr{F}\otimes\chi

on
X
.

3.6.
The
\ell
-adic sheaves isomorphism

The main application of this theorem in our paper is to the situation where
X
is a (modular) curve of genus 0 defined over
\mathbf{Q}

and the two local systems are an automorphic sheaf and a hypergeometric sheaf respectively. The isomorphism of local systems
on the analytic space
X^{\mathrm{an}}
results from rigidity, as shown in §
3.4
. Because our local systems arise from geometry, they have special properties. For instance, they belong to a strictly compatible family of rational
\ell
-adic representations.

To determine the twisting character
\chi
in the statement of the Comparison Theorem
8
it suffices
to restrict the sheaves to a
\mathbf{Q}
-rational point of the curve
X^{\circ}
. Then
\mathscr{F}
and

\mathscr{G}
define representations of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}:=\mathrm{Gal}(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}/\mathbf{Q})
. The twisting character
\chi
in the statement of the Comparison Theorem
8
is
an algebraic Hecke character of the number field
\mathbf{Q}
. The reader is referred to Serre
[
62
]
for more detail.

In particular, we recall Corollary 2.1.6 of
[
9
]
.

Lemma 3
.

If
\chi

is a 1-dimensional representation of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
taking values in the group of units of a finite extension of
\mathbf{Q}_{\ell}
,
which is unramified almost everywhere and whose restriction to the decomposition group at
\ell
is crystalline, then
\chi=\epsilon_{\ell}^{m}\xi
, where
\epsilon_{\ell}
is the
\ell
-adic cyclotomic character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
,
m\in\mathbf{Z}
and
\xi
is a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
of finite order.

As we have seen in §
2
, the automorphic sheaves
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\mathbf{C}}
have
\ell
-adic counterparts
V^{k}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
. For the groups
\Gamma
we consider, we have introduced the hypergeometric sheaves
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}
attached to the hypergeometric data
HD(\Gamma)
in Tables
2
and
3
. To these
HD(\Gamma)
, Katz has associated the
\ell
-adic sheaves
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
, whose behavior at
0,1,\infty
agrees with that of
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\mathbf{C}}
under the change of generator
\lambda\mapsto 1/\lambda
(see
[
40
]
). Theorems
6
and
7
tell us how the two complex sheaves of different origin are related. Using the Comparison Theorem
8
, we know that the same relation holds for their
\ell
-adic counterparts up to twist by a character
\chi_{\Gamma}
. In the next section we will give more details of the
\ell
-adic hypergeometric sheaves and determine the character
\chi_{\Gamma}
.

4.
Hypergeometric character sums

Given a hypergeometric datum
HD=\{\alpha,\beta\}
of length
n
, in §
3
we associated the hypergeometric function
F(HD;t)
over
\mathbf{C}

and a rank-
n
sheaf
\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\mathbf{C}}
on
{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus\{0,1,\infty\}

along with the monodromy representation of the fundamental group
\pi_{1}({\mathbb{P}}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\smallsetminus\{0,1,\infty\},*)
with a base point. When the elements in
\alpha,\beta
are in
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
and
HD
is primitive, for each prime
\ell
, Katz in
[
40
,
41
]
has constructed an
\ell
-adic sheaf
\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\ell}
of rank-
n
on
\mathbb{G}_{m}
together with the action of the absolute Galois group
G(M)=Gal(\bar{\mathbf{Q}}/\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M}))
of the cyclotomic field
\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})
. Here
M=M(HD)=lcd(\alpha\cup\beta)
, the least positive common denominator of elements in
\alpha
and
\beta
, is the level of
HD
. After changing the generator
\lambda
by
1/\lambda
, this is the
\ell
-adic counterpart of the complex hypergeometric sheaf
\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\mathbf{C}}
. The Galois action on each stalk of
\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\ell}
is described by its Frobenius traces which are given by hypergeometric character sums expressed either in terms of the period functions
{\mathbb{P}}(HD)
over finite fields introduced in
[
27
]
or the
H_{q}(HD)
-functions introduced by Beukers-Cohen-Mellit in
[
16
]
based on McCarthy’s work
[
52
]
. These will be recalled below.

4.1.
Hypergeometric functions over finite fields

Let
\mathbf{F}_{q}
be a finite field of odd characteristic and use
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
to denote the group of multiplicative characters of
\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}
.
We use
\varepsilon_{q}
or simply
\varepsilon
to denote the trivial character, and
\phi_{q}
or
\phi
to denote the quadratic character. For any
A\in\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
, use
\overline{A}
to denote its inverse, and extend
A
to
\mathbf{F}_{q}
by setting

A(0)=0
. For any characters
A_{i}
,
B_{i}\in\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
,
i=1,\cdots,n
with
B_{1}=\varepsilon
in
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
, the
{}_{n}\mathbb{P}_{n-1}
-function (cf.
[
27
]
) is defined as follows:

(30)

\,_{n}{\mathbb{P}}_{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}A_{1}&A_{2}&\cdots&A_{n}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&B_{2}&\cdots&B_{n}\end{matrix}\;;\;\lambda;q\right]\\
:=\prod_{i=2}^{n}\left(-A_{i}B_{i}(-1)\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{q-1}\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}}\binom{A_{1}\chi}{\chi}\binom{A_{2}\chi}{B_{2}\chi}\cdots\binom{A_{n}\chi}{B_{n}\chi}\chi(\lambda)+\delta(\lambda)\prod_{i=2}^{n}\binom{A_{i}}{B_{i}}\right),

where

\displaystyle\binom{A}{B}:=-B(-1)J(A,\overline{B}),\quad J(A,B):=\sum_{t\in\mathbf{F}_{q}}A(t)B(1-t),\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\delta(\lambda):=\begin{cases}1,&\mbox{ if }\lambda=0,\\
0,&\mbox{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Let
\alpha=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\},
and
\beta=\{1,b_{2},\cdots,b_{n}\}
be multi-sets with entries in
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
. Denote the positive least common denominator of all
a_{i},b_{j}
by
M:=\mathrm{lcd}(\alpha\cup\beta)
.
For a finite field
\mathbf{F}_{q}
containing a primitive
M
th root of
1

and any
\lambda\in\mathbf{F}_{q}
, let
\omega
be a generator of
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
. Following
[
49
]
, we write

(31)

\mathbb{P}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\mathbf{F}_{q};\omega):=\,_{n}{\mathbb{P}}_{n-1}\left[\begin{matrix}\omega^{(q-1)a_{1}}&\omega^{(q-1)a_{2}}&\cdots&\omega^{(q-1)a_{n}}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&\omega^{(q-1)b_{2}}&\cdots&\omega^{(q-1)b_{n}}\end{matrix}\;;\;\lambda;\mathbf{F}_{q}\right].

Write
G_{K}=\mathrm{Gal}(\overline{\mathbf{Q}}/K)
for the absolute Galois group of a number field
K
. When
K=\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})
where
\zeta_{M}
is a primitive
M
th root of unity, we abbreviate
G_{\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})}
by
G(M)
. Note that the Frobenius conjugacy classes
\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp}
over the degree-1 prime ideals
\wp
of
\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})
are dense in
G(M)
, hence irreducible Galois representations of
G(M)
are uniquely determined by their Frobenius traces on almost all degree-1 prime ideals
\wp
, which are those above the primes
p\equiv 1\mod M
. Denote by
\chi_{d}
the quadratic character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
corresponding to the quadratic field
\mathbf{Q}(\sqrt{d})
.

The following frequently used fact concerning extensions of a Galois representation results from the Frobenius reciprocity law.

Theorem 9
.

Let
K
be a finite Galois extension of
\mathbf{Q}
and
\sigma
be a finite dimensional irreducible
\ell
-adic representation of
G_{K}
.
If for every
g\in G_{\mathbf{Q}}
, the conjugate
\sigma^{g}\cong\sigma
as
G_{K}
-modules, then
\sigma
can be extended to a representation of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
, which is unique up to a linear character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
trivial on
G_{K}
.

Now we describe the stalks of an
\ell
-adic hypergeometric sheaf.

Theorem 10

(Katz
[
40
,
41
]
)
.

Let
\ell
be a prime. Given a primitive hypergeometric datum HD =
\{\alpha,\beta\}
consisting of
\alpha=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\}
,
\beta=\{1,b_{2},\cdots,b_{n}\}
with
a_{i},b_{j}\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
and
M:=M(HD)=\mathrm{lcd}(\alpha\cup\beta)
,
for each
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}[\zeta_{M},1/M]\smallsetminus\{0\}
the following hold for the
\ell
-adic representation
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}
of
G(M)
on the stalk at
\lambda
of the hypergeometric sheaf
\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\ell}
:

i).

\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}
is unramified
at
the prime ideals
\wp
of
\mathbf{Z}[\zeta_{M},1/(M\ell\lambda)]
such that its trace at the conjugacy class
\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp}
of the geometric Frobenius at
\wp
in
G(M)
with residue field
\kappa_{\wp}
and norm
N(\wp)=|\kappa_{\wp}|
is

(32)

\mathrm{Tr}\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})=(-1)^{n-1}{\omega_{\wp}^{(N(\wp)-1)a_{1}}(-1)}{\mathbb{P}}(HD;1/\lambda;\kappa_{\wp};{\omega_{\wp}}),

where

\omega_{\wp}
is a generator of
\widehat{\kappa_{\wp}^{\times}}
satisfying
\omega_{\wp}(\zeta_{M}\mod\wp)=\zeta_{M}^{-1}
.

ii).

When
\lambda\neq 1
, the stalk
(\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}
has dimension

n
and all roots of the characteristic polynomial of
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})
are algebraic numbers and have the same absolute value
N(\wp)^{(n-1)/2}
under all archimedean embeddings.

iii).

When
\lambda=1
, the dimension of
(\mathcal{H}(HD)_{\ell})_{\bar{\lambda}}
equals
n-1
.

Remark 4.

Recall that for
a=r/M
with
r\in\mathbf{Z}
,
\omega_{\wp}^{(N(\wp)-1)a}
is the character
\iota_{\wp}(a)=\left((\frac{\cdot}{\wp})_{M}\right)^{r}
, the
r
th power of the
M
th norm residue symbol at a nonzero unramified prime ideal
\wp
as in Definition 5.9 of
[
27
]
. In particular, when
\wp
varies, the sign
\omega_{\wp}^{(N(\wp)-1)a}(-1){=\iota_{\wp}(a)(-1)}
defines a character of
G(M)
, denoted by
\phi(M,a)
.
As the character
\phi(M,a)
is invariant under conjugation by
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G(M)
, it
extends to a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
and our convention here is use the extension with minimal conductor. It is nontrivial only when
{\rm ord}_{2}~M=-{\rm ord}_{2}~a=r\geq 1
.
Especially, when
{\rm ord}_{2}~M=-{\rm ord}_{2}~a=1
,
\phi(M,a)(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\left(\frac{-1}{p}\right)
is given by the Legendre symbol at odd primes
p
.

4.2.
Beukers-Cohen-Mellit’s finite Hypergeometric functions

For
q\equiv 1\mod M
, let

H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\omega):=\frac{1}{1-q}\sum_{k=0}^{q-2}\omega^{k}((-1)^{n}\lambda)\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\mathfrak{g}(\omega^{(q-1)a_{j}+k})}{\mathfrak{g}(\omega^{(q-1)a_{j}})}\frac{\mathfrak{g}(\overline{\omega}^{(q-1)b_{j}+k})}{\mathfrak{g}(\overline{\omega}^{(q-1)b_{j}})},

where
\mathfrak{g}(\chi):=\sum_{x\in\mathbf{F}_{q}}\Psi(x)\chi(x)
denotes the Gauss sum of
\chi
with respect to a fixed choice of non-trivial additive character
\Psi
of
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. However the above sum is independent of the choice of
\Psi
since
|\alpha|=|\beta|
.
The function
H_{q}
is
equivalent to the normalized
{\mathbb{P}}
-function (denoted by
\mathbb{F}
-function in
[
27
]
)
when the pair
\alpha
and
\beta
is primitive, in which case they are related by

(33)

{\mathbb{P}}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\mathbf{F}_{q};\omega)=H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\omega)\cdot\prod_{i=2}^{n}\omega^{(q-1)a_{i}}(-1)J(\omega^{(q-1)a_{i}},\overline{\omega}^{(q-1)b_{i}}).

By Weil
[
71
]
, as
\mathbf{F}_{q}
runs through the residue fields of almost all prime ideals of
\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})
, these Jacobi sums define a
Grössencharacter of
G(M)
. The above relation indicates that instead of using the
{\mathbb{P}}
-functions to describe the trace function of the Galois representations in Theorem
10
, one can also use the
H
-functions.

When
HD=\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
\lambda
are both defined over
\mathbf{Q}
,
one can rewrite
H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\omega)
by
using the reflection and multiplication formulas of Gauss sums
[
16
, Theorem 1.3]
:

\mathfrak{g}(A)\mathfrak{g}(\overline{A})=A(-1)q,\;\;\forall A\neq\varepsilon,\quad\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\eta\in\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}\\
\eta^{m}=\varepsilon\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathfrak{g}(A\eta)}{\mathfrak{g}(\eta)}=-\mathfrak{g}(A^{m})A(m^{-m}),\text{ when }m\mid q-1.

Firstly write
\displaystyle\prod_{j=1}^{n}\frac{X-e^{2\pi ia_{j}}}{X-e^{2\pi ib_{j}}}=\frac{\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X^{p_{j}}-1)}{\prod_{k=1}^{s}(X^{q_{k}}-1)}
where
p_{j},q_{k}\in\mathbf{Z}_{>0}
and
p_{j}\neq q_{k}
for all
j,k
. Then

(34)

H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;\lambda;\omega)=\frac{(-1)^{r+s}}{1-q}\sum_{m=0}^{q-2}q^{-s(0)+s(m)}\prod_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{g}(\omega^{mp_{j}})\prod_{k=1}^{s}\mathfrak{g}(\omega^{-mq_{k}})\omega(N^{-1}\lambda),

where

s(m)
is the multiplicity of
X-e^{\frac{2\pi im}{q-1}}
in

gcd(\prod_{j=1}^{r}(X^{p_{j}}-1),\prod_{k=1}^{s}(X^{q_{k}}-1))
, and

N=(-1)^{\sum_{k=1}^{s}q_{k}}\cdot\frac{p_{1}^{p_{1}}\cdots p_{r}^{p_{r}}}{q_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots q_{s}^{q_{s}}}.

In this case
H_{q}
is
\mathbf{Q}
-valued, and hence is independent of the choice of
\omega
, which will be dropped from the left hand side of (
34
). Also (
34
) holds now for

\mathbf{F}_{q}
as long as
q
is coprime to
lcd(\{1/M,\lambda\})
.

The next theorem follows from Theorems
10
and
9
.

Theorem 11

(Katz, Beukers-Cohen-Mellit)
.

Let
HD=\{\alpha=\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}\},\beta=\{1,b_{2},\cdots,b_{n}\}\}
be a primitive hypergeometric datum defined over
\mathbf{Q}
with level
M=M(HD)
. Let

\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}[1/M]\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Assume that exactly
m
elements in
\beta
are in
\mathbf{Z}
.
Then, for each prime
\ell
, there exists an
\ell
-adic representation
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
, unramified almost everywhere, with the following properties:

i).

\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}|_{G(M)}\cong\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}
.

ii).

For any prime
p\nmid\ell M
such that
\mathrm{ord}_{p}\lambda=0
,

(35)

\mathrm{Tr}\,\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\phi(M,\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})H_{p}(HD;1/\lambda)\cdot p^{(n-m)/2}\in\mathbf{Z},

where

\phi(M,a)
, as explained in Remark
4.1
, is a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
of order at most 2.

iii).

When
\lambda=1
,
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is
(n-1)
-dimensional and it has a subrepresentation, denoted by
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM,prim}
, of dimension
2\lfloor\frac{n-1}{2}\rfloor
.
For each unramified prime
p
, all roots of the characteristic polynomial of
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM,prim}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})
have absolute value
p^{(n-1)/2}
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
11
, the relation (
33
) at a prime
\wp
of
\mathbf{Z}[\zeta_{M}]
above a prime
p\equiv 1\mod M
simplifies as
\mathbb{P}(HD;\lambda;\kappa_{\wp};\omega_{\wp})=H_{p}(HD;\lambda;\omega_{\wp})p^{\frac{n-m}{2}}\phi(M,\sum_{i=2}^{n}a_{i})(-1)^{n-1}
. This fact is incorporated in the expression of (
35
).

Under the above assumption, we now clarify the relation between
H_{p}(\alpha,\beta;1/\lambda)
and
H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;1/\lambda)
where
q=p^{r}
. Let
\phi(M,\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})p^{(n-m)/2}u_{i},1\leq i\leq d,
be the roots of the characteristic polynomial of
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})
so that
H_{p}(\alpha,\beta;1/\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}
. Here
d=n
for
\lambda\neq 1
, and
d=n-1
for
\lambda=1
. Then
\mathrm{Tr}\,\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}((\mathrm{Frob}_{p})^{r})=\phi(M,\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})((\mathrm{Frob}_{p})^{r})q^{(n-m)/2}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{r}
. On the other hand, let
K
be a finite Galois extension of
\mathbb{Q}
unramified at
p
such that any prime ideal
\wp
of
K
above
p
has residue field
\mathbf{F}_{q}
. Then

\mathrm{Tr}\,\left(\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}\right)|_{G_{K}}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})=\phi(M,\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i})(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})H_{q}(\alpha,\beta;1/\lambda)\cdot q^{(n-m)/2}.

Since
\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp}=(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})^{r}
, we conclude that
H_{p^{r}}(\alpha,\beta;1/\lambda)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}u_{i}^{r}.

Remark 5.

When
HD
and
\lambda
are both defined over
\mathbf{Q}
,
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is an extension of
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}
from
G(M)
to
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
, which is unique up to a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G(M)\cong{\rm Gal}(\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{M})/\mathbf{Q})
when
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}
is an irreducible
G(M)
-module. Thus on each
G(M)
-coset in
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
,
\rho_{HD,\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is determined by the action at one Frobenius element belonging to that coset. This property is used to extend identities involving the
{\mathbb{P}}
-functions to identities involving the
H_{p}
-functions.

4.3.
For groups in a) list

We now relate the groups of the form
\Gamma=(\infty,\infty,e_{\infty})
in Figure
1(a)
and Table
2
to elliptic curves. In the table below
t
is the Hauptmodul of
\Gamma
the same as
\lambda
in Theorem 3, and
E_{\Gamma,t}

X_{\Gamma}
with
t\neq 0,1,\infty
.

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr e_{\infty}&E_{\Gamma,t}&HD(\Gamma)&j_{\Gamma}(t)&c_{4}(\mathcal{E}_{j_{\Gamma}(t))})/c_{4}(E_{\Gamma,t})\\
\hline\cr\infty&\displaystyle y^{2}+xy+\frac{t}{16}y=x^{3}+\frac{t}{16}x^{2}&\{\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\},\{1,1\}\}&\displaystyle\frac{16(t^{2}-16t+16)^{3}}{t^{4}(1-t)}&\left(\frac{4(t^{2}-16t+16)}{(t-2)(t^{2}+32t-32)}\right)^{2}\\

The universal elliptic curve has been recalled in (
25
). Both
\mathcal{E}_{j_{\Gamma}(t)}
and
E_{\Gamma,t}
have the same
j
-invariant and their
c_{4}
-constants quotients are squares.
Thus
E_{\Gamma,t}

is isomorphic to the pullback of the universal family
\mathcal{E}_{j}

via the covering map
j_{\Gamma}(t)
.

Based on
[
43
]
by Koike and
[
57
]
by Ono (who used a model isogenous to the one above over
\mathbf{Q}(t)
) for the case
e_{\infty}=\infty
and
[
16
]
by Beukers-Cohen-Mellit for the case
e_{\infty}=3
, for
t\in\mathbf{Q}\setminus\{0,1\}

and any prime
p>3
not dividing the level of
E_{\Gamma,t}
, we have

(36)

\#E_{\Gamma,t}(\mathbf{F}_{p})=p+1-H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);t).

For any elliptic curve defined over
\mathbf{Q}
and prime
\ell
, the determinant of the associated
\ell
-adic representation of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
is the cyclotomic character, denoted by
\epsilon_{\ell}
. Putting together, one has

Proposition 4
.

Let
\Gamma=(\infty,\infty,e_{\infty})
where
e_{\infty}\in\{3,\infty\}
.
Given
t\in\mathbf{Q}\setminus\{0,1\}
and prime
\ell
, at any prime
p\neq\ell
where the 2-dimensional representation

\rho^{BCM}_{HD(\Gamma),1/t,\ell}
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
is unramified, the characteristic polynomial of
\rho^{BCM}_{HD(\Gamma),1/t,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})
is

T^{2}-H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);t)T+p.

Proposition
4
implies, for each
\Gamma
in a) list, the isomorphism between the stalks
(\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell})_{\bar{\frac{1}{t}}}
of the hypergeometric sheaf and
(V^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell})_{\bar{t}}
of the automorphic sheaf at
t\in\mathbf{Q}\setminus\{0,1\}
.

This Proposition also holds for
\Gamma=(\infty,\infty,2)
with
E_{\Gamma,t}:y^{2}=x(x^{2}+x+\frac{t}{4})
and
HD(\Gamma)^{\prime}=\{\{\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\},\{1,1\}\}
such that at almost all primes
p
, the characteristic polynomial of
\rho^{BCM}_{HD(\Gamma)^{\prime},1/t,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})
is
T^{2}-H_{p}(HD(\Gamma)^{\prime};t)T+p
, as shown in
[
16
]
.

Remark 6.
In the elliptic modular cases, we can define
V^{1}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
even when
\Gamma
contains
-I_{2}
, as

R^{1}f_{*}\bar{\mathbf{Q}}_{\ell}
for a suitable family of elliptic curves
f:E\to X_{\Gamma}^{{\circ}}
, not unique in general.
As Scholl proves, for computing the Hecke traces for modular forms of even weight, any such suitable
family of elliptic curves will do. By suitable we mean that
the
j
-invariant of
E\to X_{\Gamma}
is the given map
X_{\Gamma}\to X_{\Gamma(1)}
and model is defined over
\mathbb{Q}(j)
. See
[
60
, Prop. 1.3]
.

4.4.
For groups in b) list

Throughout the remaining discussion of this section,
we assume all hypergeometric data under consideration are primitive for simplicity.

We begin by recalling the character sum analogue of the Clausen formula (
26
) established by Evans-Greene
[
24
]
, and rephrased in a way compatible with our current setting in
[
49
]
. To ease the notation, we use
J_{\omega}(a,b)
to denote
J(\omega^{(q-1)a},\omega^{(q-1)b})
below.

Theorem 12

(Evans-Greene
[
24
]
)
.

Let
a,b,s\in\mathbf{Q}
such that
2s\equiv a+b\mod\mathbf{Z}
. Let
q
be a prime power which is congruent to 1 modulo
\mathrm{lcd}\{a,b,s,\frac{1}{2}+b-s\}
and
\omega
a generator of
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
.
For any

t\in\mathbf{F}_{q}\setminus\{0,1\}
, we have

(37)

H_{q}\left(\{-\frac{1}{2}+b-s,s\},\{1,b\};{t;\omega}\right)H_{q}\left(\{-\frac{1}{2}-b+s,1-s\},\{1,1-b\};{t;\omega}\right)\\
=\phi_{q}(1-t)H_{q}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},a,1-a\},\{1,b,1-b\};{t;\omega}\right)+q^{\delta(b)},

where
\delta(b)=1
if
b\in\mathbf{Z}
and
0
otherwise.

When
t=1
, we have

(38)

H_{q}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},a,1-a\},\{1,b,1-b\};{1;\omega}\right)\\
=\frac{1}{q^{1-\delta(b)}}\frac{J_{\omega}(a+b,b-a)}{J_{\omega}(\frac{1}{2},-b)}\left(J_{\omega}(s-b,\frac{1}{2}-s)^{2}+J_{\omega}(\frac{1}{2}+s-b,s)^{2}\right),

if
\omega^{(q-1)(a+b)}
is a square in
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{q}^{\times}}
; otherwise

(39)

H_{q}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},a,1-a\},\{1,b,1-b\};{1;\omega}\right)=0.

By Prop. 8.7 of
[
27
]
, the two
H_{q}
-functions on the left side of (
37
) are related by

(40)

H_{q}\left(\{-\frac{1}{2}+b-s,s\},\{1,b\};{t;\omega}\right)\\
=\overline{\omega}^{(q-1)b}(t)\phi_{q}(1-t)\frac{J_{\omega}(-s,s-b)}{J_{\omega}(\frac{1}{2}+s,\frac{1}{2}+b-s)}H_{q}\left(\{-\frac{1}{2}-b+s,1-s\},\{1,1-b\};{t;\omega}\right).

For each group
\Gamma
in Theorem
1
, the hypergeometric datum
HD(\Gamma)=\{\alpha(\Gamma),\beta(\Gamma)\}
is self-dual. Thus one can find a (non-unique) pair
(a,b)
such that
\alpha(\Gamma)=\{\frac{1}{2},a,1-a\},\beta(\Gamma)=\{1,b,1-b\}
.
With
s=\frac{a+b-1}{2}
, the hypergeometric datum
HD_{1}(\Gamma):=\{\{-\frac{1}{2}+b-s,s\},\{1,b\}\}
on the left hand side of (
37
)
agrees with the datum associated to the
{}_{2}F_{1}
listed in the second to the last column of Table
3
.

Proof.

By Remark
4.1
, the characters
\phi(M(HD_{1}(\Gamma)),\frac{1}{2})
and
\phi(M(HD(\Gamma)),\frac{1}{2})
are trivial on
G(N(\Gamma))
,
so they will not show up in the discussion below. Since a prime
p\equiv 1\mod N(\Gamma)
splits completely in
\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{N(\Gamma)})
, the prime ideals
\wp
above

have norm equal to
p
. The trace of
\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})
follows from (
32
) and (
33
).
Statement 1) follows from (
40
) while statement 2) follows from the finite field Clausen formula (Theorem
12
). More precisely, when
b=1
, the quotient of the Jacobi sums on the right hand side of (
40
) is 1.
In the case of
(2,4,6)
, letting

and

in (

) gives the following trace function for
\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})

\displaystyle=-J_{\omega}\left(\frac{17}{24},\frac{5}{6}\right)H_{p}\left(\{\frac{17}{24},\frac{23}{24}\},\{1,\frac{7}{6}\};1/\lambda;\omega_{\wp}\right)

\displaystyle=-\phi_{p}(1-1/\lambda)\overline{\omega}_{\wp}(1/\lambda)^{\frac{p-1}{6}}\cdot C(\Gamma,\omega_{\wp})\cdot J_{\omega}\left(\frac{7}{24},-\frac{5}{6}\right)H_{p}(HD_{1}(\Gamma);1/\lambda;\omega_{\wp})

\displaystyle=\phi_{p}(1-1/\lambda)\overline{\omega}_{\wp}(1/\lambda)^{\frac{p-1}{6}}\cdot C(\Gamma,\omega_{\wp})\cdot\mathrm{Tr}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})

where
C(\Gamma,\omega_{\wp})=\frac{J_{\omega}(\frac{17}{24},\frac{5}{6})}{J_{\omega}(\frac{7}{24},-\frac{5}{6})}\frac{J_{\omega}(\frac{7}{24},\frac{13}{24})}{J_{\omega}(\frac{5}{24},-\frac{1}{24})}
. By converting
J(A,B)=\mathfrak{g}(A)\mathfrak{g}(B)/\mathfrak{g}(AB)
when
AB
is nontrivial and using identities on Gauss sums, one verifies that
C(\Gamma,\omega_{\wp})=1.

Thus
\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\cong\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\chi_{1-1/\lambda}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\otimes\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
, where
\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
is a finite order character of
G_{N(\Gamma)}
; this is 1). Consequently,

\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}={\chi_{1-\frac{1}{\lambda}}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes(\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell})\cong{\chi_{1-\frac{1}{\lambda}}}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\left(\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\oplus\mathrm{Alt}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\right),

showing that
\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
contains the degree-3 subrepresentation
\chi_{1-1/\lambda}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
. On the other hand, it follows from the Frobenius traces of
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}
shown in Table
5
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\bar{\sigma}_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})

\displaystyle=p^{1-\delta(b)}H_{p}(HD_{1}(\Gamma);1/\lambda;\omega_{\wp})H_{p}(HD_{1}^{\prime}(\Gamma);1/\lambda;\omega_{\wp})

\displaystyle=p^{1-\delta(b)}\phi_{p}(1-1/\lambda)H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1/\lambda;\omega_{\wp})+p

\displaystyle=\chi_{1-1/\lambda}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})\mathrm{Tr}\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp})+\epsilon_{\ell}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\wp}).

In the above formula, the second equality follows from the Clausen formula (
37
), and the third equality results from Theorem
10
and the relation (
33
). This proves that
\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\cong\chi_{1-1/\lambda}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}

and
\epsilon_{\ell}\cong\chi_{1-1/\lambda}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Alt}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}
, which in turn implies that

\det\left(\chi_{1-1/\lambda}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\xi_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\otimes\mathrm{Sym}^{2}\sigma_{\Gamma,\lambda,\ell}\right)=\det\left(\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}|_{G(N(\Gamma))}\right)=\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}.

This proves 2).
∎

Proposition
5
is the hypergeometric sheaf version of Proposition
3
. For each
\Gamma
in Proposition
5
, the hypergeometric datum
HD(\Gamma)
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
so that the representation
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}
for
\lambda
in
\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
can be extended to
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
; we choose
\rho^{BCM}_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}
as its extension. This defines
the
\ell
-adic hypergeometric sheaf
\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
on
X_{\Gamma}
. Recall from definition
2
that the characters
\chi_{1-1/\lambda}
come from the sheaf
\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}
on
X_{\Gamma}
.
These representations have
\mathbf{Q}
-valued traces. Other extensions differ from this by a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G({N(\Gamma)})
. By the Isomorphism Theorem
7
over
\mathbf{C}
and the Comparison Theorem
8
,
\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
(upon changing
\lambda
to
1/\lambda
) and
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
differ by a character
\chi_{\Gamma}
of
G_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Both sheaves are punctually pure of the same weight, which can be seen from
Propositions
3
and

5
. Thus, by Lemma
3

\chi_{\Gamma}
has finite order. From definition, the traces of
\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
at rational stalks have values in
\mathbf{Q}
. The traces of
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
at rational stalks, which originated in the local zeta functions of abelian varieties as in
[
44
]
, also have values in
\mathbf{Q}
(See (
19
)). Thus the order of
\chi_{\Gamma}
is at most 2.
We summarize the discussion in the following Corollary.

Corollary 2
.

\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
(upon changing
\lambda
by
1/\lambda
) and
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
differ by a character
\chi_{\Gamma}
of order at most 2.

Proposition 6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group in Table
5
with datum
HD(\Gamma)=\{\alpha(\Gamma),\beta(\Gamma)\}
. Write
\varphi_{\Gamma}
for the character
\phi(lcd(HD(\Gamma)),\sum_{a_{i}\in\alpha(\Gamma)}a_{i})
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
in Theorem
11
ii).
For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}\setminus\{0,1\}
,
the determinant of
\varphi_{\Gamma}\otimes\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is
\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
for the first 4 groups and is
\chi_{-3}\otimes\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
for
(2,4,6)
.

Proof.

Recall that
X_{\Gamma}
is a genus zero curve defined over
\mathbf{Q}
. Using the model we choose, we identify
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}(\mathbf{C})
with
\mathbb{P}_{\mathbf{C}}^{1}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. The representation
\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is the Galois action on the stalk at
\lambda
of the rank-3 sheaf
\mathcal{L}(\Gamma)_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
,
so its determinant, as
\lambda
varies, constitutes a rank-1
\ell
-adic sheaf, denoted by
\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
, on
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Q}}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
. According to Proposition
5
, when restricted to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{N(\Gamma)})}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
,

\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
is the constant sheaf
\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
. Therefore

\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
will be a constant sheaf with value
\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
times a character
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G(N(\Gamma))
by Theorem
8
.
As
\varphi_{\Gamma}
is also a constant degree-1 sheaf on
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Q}}\setminus\{0,1,\infty\}
, trivial on
G({N(\Gamma)})
by Remark
4.1
,
this proves that the determinant of
\varphi_{\Gamma}\otimes{\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes}\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
is
\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
up to a character
\psi_{\lambda}
trivial on
G({N(\Gamma)})
and independent of
\lambda
. By Theorem
11
ii) the trace of
\varphi_{\Gamma}\otimes{\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes}\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})
is
\left(\frac{1-1/\lambda}{p}\right)H_{p}(HD(\Gamma),1/\lambda)p^{(3-m)/2}
for primes
p\nmid{N(\Gamma)}\ell
. Here
m
is the number of
1
’s in
\beta(\Gamma)
.

Let
S
be the group of characters of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G({N(\Gamma)})\cong\mathrm{Gal}(\mathbf{Q}(\zeta_{{N(\Gamma)}})/\mathbf{Q})
, which is a product of finitely many copies of
\mathbf{Z}/2\mathbf{Z}
. Hence any character in
S
has order at most 2. We proceed to show that
\psi_{\lambda}
is
\chi_{-3}
for
\Gamma=(2,4,6)
and trivial otherwise.
As it is independent of
\lambda
, it suffices to choose a convenient
\lambda
and compute the determinant of the representation at a few primes using the
Magma
hypergeometric motive package implemented by Watkins
[
70
]
to
make the final conclusion.

For
(2,\infty,\infty)
,
N(\Gamma)=4
,
S=\langle\chi_{\pm 1}\rangle
.
It suffices to consider
\lambda=3
at
p=7
; the determinant is
7^{3}
. Hence
\psi_{\lambda}
is trivial.

For
(2,3,\infty)
and for
(2,6,\infty)
,
N(\Gamma)=12
,
S=\langle\chi_{\pm 1},\chi_{\pm 3}\rangle
. It suffices to consider
\lambda=3
at
p=7,11
; the determinants are
7^{3}
and
11^{3}
, respectively. Hence
\psi_{\lambda}
is trivial.

For
(2,4,\infty)
,
N(\Gamma)=8
, the final choice is among trivial,
S=\langle\chi_{\pm 1},\chi_{\pm 2}\rangle
. It suffices to consider
\lambda=3
at
p=5,7,11
; the determinants are
5^{3}
,
7^{3}
and
11^{3}
, respectively. Hence
\psi_{\lambda}
is trivial.

For
(2,4,6)
,
M(\Gamma)=24
,
S=\langle\chi_{\pm 1},\chi_{\pm 2},\chi_{\pm 3}\rangle
. It suffices to consider
\lambda=3
at
p=5
,
7
,
11
,
13
,
17
,
19
, and the corresponding determinants are
-5^{3},7^{3},-11^{3},13^{3},

-17^{3},

19^{3}
from which we conclude
\psi_{\lambda}=\chi_{-3}.

∎

A straightforward computation shows that the quadratic character
\varphi_{\Gamma}
in Proposition
6
is equal to
\chi_{-1}
for
\Gamma=(2,\infty,\infty),(2,3,\infty)
and
(2,6,\infty)
, and is trivial for
\Gamma=(2,4,\infty)
and
(2,4,6)
. It follows from Proposition
3
and the definition of
V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
as the pushforward of
V^{2}(U^{\prime})_{\ell}
for a neat normal compact open finite-index subgroup
U^{\prime}
of the group
U
corresponding to
\Gamma
that, at a
\mathbf{Q}
-rational point
\lambda
of
X_{\Gamma}^{\circ}
,
\det\rho^{2}(\Gamma)_{\lambda,\ell}=\epsilon_{\ell}^{3}
. This also can be seen from the fact that corresponding modular forms have trivial central characters. The representation at the stalk at
\lambda
of
\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}
is
\chi_{1-1/\lambda}\otimes\rho_{HD(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}^{BCM}
, which is isomorphic to
\chi_{\Gamma}\otimes\rho^{2}(\Gamma)_{\lambda,\ell}
for a character
\chi_{\Gamma}
of order
\leq 2
by Corollary
2
. The above proposition then determines
\chi_{\Gamma}^{3}=\chi_{\Gamma}
. We record the conclusion below.

Theorem 13
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group in Table
5
with datum
HD(\Gamma)=\{\alpha(\Gamma),\beta(\Gamma)\}
as in Theorem
1
. Then, upon changing
\lambda
to
1/\lambda
,
\mathcal{H}(\{1/2\},\{1\})_{\ell}\otimes\mathcal{H}(HD(\Gamma))_{\ell}=\chi_{\Gamma}\otimes V^{2}(\Gamma)_{\ell}
with
\chi_{\Gamma}=\chi_{-1}
for
\Gamma=(2,\infty,\infty),(2,3,\infty)
and
(2,6,\infty)
,
\chi_{\Gamma}=\chi_{-3}
for
\Gamma=(2,4,6)
, and
\chi_{\Gamma}
trivial for
\Gamma=(2,4,\infty)
.

4.5.
Some properties of
H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);t)

We explore properties of
H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);t)
for
\Gamma
in Table
5
according to their type. The case
\Gamma=(2,4,6)
of type b.II) will be treated separately.
For the four groups
\Gamma
of type b.I),
HD(\Gamma)=\{\{\frac{1}{2},a(\Gamma),1-a(\Gamma)\},\{1,1,1\}\}
where
a(\Gamma)
is listed in Table
5
.
The discussion for these groups will be in terms of
d=d(\Gamma)=1/a(\Gamma)
, which takes values 2,3,4,6, respectively. Define
\kappa_{d}
as follows as in Table
6
.

\Gamma

(\infty,2,\infty)

(\infty,2,6)

(\infty,2,4)

(\infty,2,3)

d=d(\Gamma)

2

3

4

6

\kappa_{d}

-1

-3

-2

-1

Table 6
.
\kappa_{d}
values

Lemma 4
.

Let
\Gamma
,
d=d(\Gamma)
and
\kappa_{d}
be as in Table
6
and
HD_{2}(\Gamma)=HD_{2}(d):=\{\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d-1}{d}\},\{1,1\}\}
.
For any prime
p>5
,
x\in\mathbf{F}_{p}\setminus\{0,1\}
, the following identities hold:

(41)

H_{p}\left(HD_{2}(d);x\right)=\left(\frac{\kappa_{d}}{p}\right)H_{p}\left(HD_{2}(d);1-x\right),

(42)

H_{p}\left(HD_{2}(d);1/2\right)=0\quad\mbox{ if }\left(\frac{\kappa_{d}}{p}\right)=-1.

Remark 7.
Since
HD_{2}(\Gamma)
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
, for
\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
, the representation
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),\lambda,\ell}
of
G(d)
can be extended to
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
. It follows from Theorem
11

and (
42
) that
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}
has CM by
\mathbf{Q}(\sqrt{\kappa_{d}})
.

Proof.

By (9.13) of
[
20
]
, these identities hold when
p\equiv 1\mod d
. For
d\in\{3,4,6\}
, by Remark
4.2

both sides agree up to a sign for
p\equiv-1\mod d
. To decide the sign, we use the following three-step strategy.

Firstly, we relate the
H_{p}
function to truncated hypergeometric series. Theorem 4 of
[
51
]
says for
d\in\{3,4,6\}
and
x\in\mathbf{F}_{p}\setminus\{0,1\}
,

H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d-1}{d}\},\{1,1\};x\right)\equiv\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{d}&\frac{d-1}{d}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;x\right]_{p-1}\mod p.

Next, we find a terminating series which is
p
-adically close to the truncated series. Let
p_{0}
be the least positive integer such that
n=\frac{pp_{0}-1}{d}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that
n\equiv-\frac{1}{d}\mod p
and
(-1)^{k}\binom{n}{k}=\frac{(-n)_{k}}{k!}\equiv\frac{(\frac{1}{d})_{k}}{k!}\mod p
,
\binom{n+k}{k}=\frac{(1+n)_{k}}{k!}\equiv\frac{(\frac{d-1}{d})_{k}}{k!}\mod p
for
0\leq k<p
. Thus

\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{d}&\frac{d-1}{d}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;x\right]_{p-1}\equiv\,_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}-n&n+1\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;x\right]\equiv\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\binom{n+k}{k}(-x)^{k}\mod p.

At last we use identities for the terminating series to reach the goal. Presently, recall that

\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\binom{n+k}{k}(-x)^{k}=(-1)^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\binom{n+k}{k}(x-1)^{k}

(see (2.3.11) of
[
6
]
). For
d=3
, when
p=6k+1
(resp.
6k-1
) for
k\in\mathbf{Z}
,
n=\frac{p-1}{3}=2k
(resp.
n=\frac{2p-1}{3}=4k-1
). Thus
(-1)^{n}=\left(\frac{-3}{p}\right).
The other cases can be verified similarly.
∎

Remark 8.
In the proof above, a congruence relation between a certain
H_{p}
function and a truncated hypergeometric series is used. Along a similar vein, the paper
[
12
]
by Barbei, Swisher, Tobin and Tu describes certain supercongruences satisfied by truncated Ramanujan-Sato series by drawing connections to arithmetic triangle groups. The supercongruences can be predicted by the properties of
H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);t)
described in this section and the recent development, which is recorded in
[
12
, Appendix]
.

The next Lemma is a special version of the Clausen formula
that works for almost all
q
.

Lemma 5
.

For
\Gamma
of type b.I),
d=d(\Gamma)\in\{2,3,4,6\}
,
p
being any prime not dividing
2d
,
and
t\in\mathbf{F}_{p}\setminus\{0,1,1/2\}
, we have

(43)

H_{p}(HD_{2}(\Gamma);t)^{2}=H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{d},1-\frac{1}{d}\},\{1,1\};t\right)^{2}=H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);4t(1-t))+p.

Proof.

By our assumption,

1-4t(1-t)=(1-2t)^{2}\neq 0,1
. For
p\equiv 1\mod 2d
, we have

\displaystyle H_{p}

\displaystyle\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{d},1-\frac{1}{d}\},\{1,1,1\};4t(1-t)\right)+p

\displaystyle\overset{Clausen}{=}H_{p}\left(\{1-\frac{1}{2d},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2d}\},\{1,1\};4t(1-t)\right)H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2d},\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2d}\},\{1,1\};4t(1-t)\right)

\displaystyle{=}H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{d},1-\frac{1}{d}\},\{1,1\};t\right)^{2}.

In the above, we use
[
27
, (9.12)]
to get the last equality.
For
p
with
p\not\equiv 1\mod 2d
, the conclusion can be drawn using the three-step strategy outlined in the proof of Lemma
4
that

H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{d},1-\frac{1}{d}\},\{1,1,1\};t\right)\equiv\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{d}&\frac{d-1}{d}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]_{p-1}\mod p.

and

\,{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{d}&\frac{d-1}{d}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1\end{matrix}\;;\;t\right]_{p-1}^{2}\equiv\,_{3}F_{2}\left[\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}&\frac{1}{d}&\frac{d-1}{d}\vskip 3.0pt plus 1.0pt minus 1.0pt\\
&1&1\end{matrix}\;;\;4t(1-t)\right]_{p-1}\mod p^{2}.

In the last step, Clausen formula over
\mathbf{C}
is used.
∎

4.6.
Special fiber at
1

In this subsection we give a description for
H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)
. This information will be useful for computing the contributions at the special fibers in §
5
. Firstly, we give an evaluation formula for the length-2 datum at
t=1/2
.

Lemma 6
.

Let
a,s\in\mathbf{Q}^{\times}
such that
2s\equiv a\mod\mathbf{Z}
. For any prime
p
congruent to 1 modulo
\mathrm{lcd}\{a,\frac{1}{2}\}
,

H_{p}\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};\frac{1}{2}\right)=-\phi_{p}(-1)\cdot\left(J_{\omega}(s,\frac{1}{2}-s)+J_{\omega}(-s,\frac{1}{2}+s)\right),

when
2|(p-1)a
; otherwise

H_{p}\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};{\frac{1}{2};\omega}\right)=0.

Proof.

When
\omega^{(p-1)a}
is a not square in
\widehat{F_{q}^{\times}}
, the evaluation formula
[
30
, (4.5)]
gives

H_{p}\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};{\frac{1}{2};\omega}\right)=0.

If
\omega^{(p-1)a}
is a square in
\widehat{\mathbf{F}_{p}^{\times}}
,
combining the evaluation formula
[
30
, (4.5)]
and the Jacobi sum identities
[
27
, (2.16), Lemma 2.8]
, we obtain firstly by
[
30
, (4.5)]

\displaystyle H_{p}\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};\frac{1}{2};\omega\right)=-\omega^{(p-1)a}(2)\cdot\left(J_{\omega}(s,-a)+J_{\omega}(\frac{1}{2}+s,-a)\right)

Further by
[
27
, Lem. 2.8]
, we get

\displaystyle H_{p}

\displaystyle\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};\frac{1}{2};\omega\right)

\displaystyle=-\omega^{(p-1)a}(2)\cdot\left(\omega^{(p-1)s}(-1)J_{\omega}(s,s)+\omega^{(p-1)(\frac{1}{2}+s)}(-1)J_{\omega}(\frac{1}{2}+s,\frac{1}{2}+s)\right)

\displaystyle=-\phi_{p}(-1)\cdot\left(J_{\omega}(s,\frac{1}{2}-s)+J_{\omega}(-s,\frac{1}{2}+s)\right).

∎

Corollary 3
.

For
\Gamma
of type b.I),
d=d(\Gamma)
and
\kappa_{d}
as in Table
6
, and any prime
p\nmid d
, we have

(44)

\displaystyle{H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)}=H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{d},\frac{d-1}{d}\},\{1,1,1\};1\right)=0,\quad{~if~}\left(\frac{\kappa_{d}}{p}\right)=-1;

(45)

\displaystyle H_{p}\left(HD_{2}(\Gamma);\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}=H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d-1}{d}\},\{1,1\};\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}=H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)+2p,\,{~~~~~if~}\left(\frac{\kappa_{d}}{p}\right)=1.

For
\Gamma=(2,4,6)

H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)=H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\},\{1,\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}\};1\right)=0
for primes
p
with
\left(\frac{-6}{p}\right)=-1
.

Proof.

For each of the five groups
\Gamma
considered here,
HD(\Gamma)
is defined over
\mathbf{Q}
, so that the degree-2 representation
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}
can be extended to
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
. It follows from Theorem
11
, ii) that at an unramified odd prime
p
, we have

{\rm Tr}\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=\phi(lcd(HD(\Gamma)),\frac{1}{2})(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})p^{u}H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)

where
u=0
(resp. 1) for
\Gamma
of type b.I) (resp. b.II)), and
{\rm Tr}\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=H_{p}(HD_{2}(\Gamma);1/2)
for
\Gamma
of type b.I). Here
HD_{2}(\Gamma)
is defined above Table
6
.

When
\Gamma
is of type b.I), by Clausen formula (
38
) with
2s\equiv a\mod\mathbf{Z}
and (
39
), for each
p\equiv 1\mod 2d
we have

H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},a,1-a\},\{1,1,1\};1\right)=J_{\omega}(s,\frac{1}{2}-s)^{2}+J_{\omega}(-s,\frac{1}{2}+s)^{2}.

This shows that the degree-2 representation
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
restricted to
G(2d)
decomposes into the sum of two distinct characters of
G(2d)
. Thus, by Clifford theory, the 2-dimensional
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{{BCM}}
is an irreducible representation induced from a one-dimensional representation
\xi
of
G(2d)
corresponding to the first Jacobi sum extended to an index-2 subgroup
H(2d)
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
generated by
G(2d)
and all
\{\tau\in G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G(2d)\mid\xi^{\tau}\cong\xi\}
. To decide
H
, we use Lemma
5
with
a=\frac{1}{d}
and
s=\frac{1}{2d}
:

(46)

H_{p}\left(\{a,1-a\},\{1,1\};\frac{1}{2}\right)^{2}=J_{\omega}(s,\frac{1}{2}-s)^{2}+J_{\omega}(-s,\frac{1}{2}+s)^{2}+2p\\
=H_{p}(HD(\Gamma);1)+2p,\quad\forall p\equiv 1\mod 2d.

This shows that the representation
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}\otimes\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}
, when restricted to
G(2d)
, agrees with the restriction to
G(2d)
of the sum of the degree-2 representation
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
and two copies of the degree-1 representation
\epsilon_{\ell}
. Since all representations involved are of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
and
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
is irreducible, we see that
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}\otimes\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}
can be decomposed in the same way, except that each irreducible component is now replaced by a suitable twist by a character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}/G(2d)
.
On the other hand, since
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}
has CM by
\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\kappa_{d}})
by Remark
4.5
, so do
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}\otimes\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}
and its only degree-2 irreducible component. Therefore
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
admits the same CM. This proves (
44
).

The identity (
45
) can be drawn by checking a few primes representing other cosets of
G(2d)
in
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
. For instance for
d=2
, we only need to check one unramified prime in
p\equiv 3\mod 4
case such as
p=7
.

For
\Gamma=(2,4,6)
,
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}
is a representation of
G(12)
. If
p\equiv 1\mod 24
, (
38
) gives

\displaystyle pH_{p}

\displaystyle\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\},\{1,\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}\};1\right)=\frac{J_{\omega}\left(\frac{5}{12},\frac{11}{12}\right)}{J_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{2},\frac{5}{6}\right)}\left(J_{\omega}\left(\frac{1}{24},\frac{7}{24}\right)^{2}+J_{\omega}\left(\frac{13}{24},\frac{19}{24}\right)^{2}\right).

By the same argument as above, the representation
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
is invariant under the twist by a quadratic character of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}
. To identify this quadratic character, it suffices to compute the values of
H_{p}\left(\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{4},\frac{3}{4}\},\{1,\frac{1}{6},\frac{5}{6}\};1\right)
for
p=5,7,11,13,17,19,23
which reveals that for these cases the value is 0 if and only if
\left(\frac{-6}{p}\right)=-1.

∎

Remark 9.

The degree-2 representations
\rho_{HD(\Gamma),1,\ell}^{BCM}
of
G_{\mathbf{Q}}

3

In Table

). Similarly for
\rho_{HD_{2}(\Gamma),2,\ell}^{BCM}

.

.

The identities in the two remaining subsections will be used in §

to identify the character
\chi_{\Gamma}
alluded to by Theorem

as mentioned in §
